Separately: the cache-save/restore failures on every run since run #1. **This Note predicted that upgrading was not expected to fix them, on the reasoning that they were GitHub-side and affected both cache consumers independently. That prediction was wrong, and the sentence is rewritten rather than annotated** — the treatment this register gives a claim that turns out wrong rather than incomplete, and the same one applied to this entry's stale version targets above. Both failures stopped in the same change: the first post-upgrade run carries **zero** annotations, and the restore, save and `Post Install uv` steps all report `success`. The "affects both consumers independently" observation was correct and its conclusion was backwards — *both* consumers were running years-old bundled `@actions/cache` clients (`actions/cache@v4` floating, `setup-uv@v3`), so one common cause explains both, and upgrading both was exactly the fix. **The lesson is the one this issue keeps relearning:** the prediction was reasoned rather than measured, it was recorded in the register where it read as established, and it took a single API call to falsify. It was at least written with the check that would settle it, which is why this correction exists at all.

### G4 — Trivy's 180s timeout has never been validated against a live DB refresh
Confirmed: M3.4, post-M3.7 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Deferral rationale: **the remedy is already written down, and has been since M3.4.** ADR-012's own Consequences names "decoupling DB refresh from per-scan latency entirely via an independent, scheduled refresh job, with every scan then passing `skip_db_update=True` against a cache that's kept warm out-of-band", and defers it as "a legitimate direction" that was not that issue's job. Given the finding below that the refresh runs *inside* the 180s budget rather than alongside it, that stops being a latency optimization and becomes the mitigation. It is deferred again here for the same reason the measurement is: both need a worker running real scans against a cold Trivy cache on production network, which does not exist before deployment. Act without waiting if a production Trivy `failure_reason` ever reads "timed out". One adjacent number is readable today: CI's `Warm Trivy vulnerability DB cache` step runs 0-6s across seventeen sampled runs (twelve on 2026-08-20/21, plus M4.1-M4.5's five on 2026-08-23/24), and `actions/cache` reports "Failed to restore: Cache service responded with 400" on every one of them (G3, re-verified against the annotations API for this entry) — so 4-6s is an upper bound on `trivy fs --download-db-only` with GitHub's cache layer contributing nothing, which bounds the happy path at single-digit seconds against 180s. It remains a proxy, not the measurement: `--download-db-only` on GitHub's network from a runner is not a refresh inside a real `trivy fs` on a production worker, and it says nothing about the case that actually matters, a slow or rate-limited registry pull.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M4 and M5, silently, on any production scan whose DB refresh runs long.** `TrivyAdapter` is wired into `platform/worker.py` with no arguments, so production gets `skip_db_update=False` (live refresh) and `timeout_seconds=180.0`, and that 180s budget covers the refresh *and* the scan in one `trivy fs` invocation — the refresh is inside the scan's budget, not additional to it. On overrun `asyncio.wait_for` raises `ScannerExecutionFailed` (`trivy_adapter.py`), which since M3.7 is caught by `_run_scanner`'s deliberately broad `except Exception` (`run_scan.py`) and recorded as `ScanResult(tool="trivy", status=FAILED)`. It never reaches arq, so **there is no retry and no backoff** — before M3.7 that same exception was in `execute`'s re-raise tuple and arq re-ran the scan, where a transient slow refresh would most likely have succeeded. The scan derives to `PARTIAL`, `get_succeeded_by_scan_id` returns Semgrep and ZAP but not Trivy, and M4 normalizes with **no SCA findings at all** while M5 correlates a view of the system with the entire dependency dimension missing — successfully, with nothing raising and nothing marking the result as incomplete beyond a status field neither module reads. Recovery requires a human to re-trigger.
Note: ADR-016 decision 1 computes `WorkerSettings.job_timeout = 600s` from "ZAP 300s, Trivy 180s, Semgrep 60s" and references ADR-012 nowhere, so the unvalidated number is now an input to a second decision. What is open is *validating* 180s, not a claim that it is wrong — ADR-012 named the issue that would validate it and that issue shipped without doing so. **`Status:` stays `open` rather than `assigned → M11.3`** deliberately: M11.3 is the earliest point at which measuring becomes *possible*, not an issue anybody has scoped this into, so this entry depends on review step 3 re-reading it at each boundary and on escalation at a third confirmation — the intended handling for something unactionable until deploy. **The proxy is unstable in both directions, and that is now measured rather than inferred. *(Corrected 2026-08-24 at the M4→M5 boundary review — this sentence previously read that the readings "split cleanly at 2026-08-21T14:01Z — 0-1s on every run before, 4-6s on every run after", concluding "4-6s is the number to trust and the 0-1s runs are unaccounted for rather than 'warm'". Five further samples falsify that: M4.1-M4.5's runs read **0s, 0s, 0s, 0s, 4s** in that order — four consecutive zeros on 2026-08-23, all of them *after* the claimed 2026-08-21 cutover and all of them in the range the old sentence said had stopped occurring, then a single 4s on 2026-08-24 — with no `ci.yml` change between any of them. So the split did not hold, and neither end of the range is the trustworthy one. Rewritten rather than annotated, on M4.4's treatment of ADR-0019's `check_id` caveat — a measurement that turns out **wrong** rather than incomplete gets its sentence replaced, because a note beside a superseded number leaves both in the entry and a reader arriving at "4-6s" has no reason to scroll.)*** What survives unchanged is the only part that was ever load-bearing: across seventeen sampled runs the step reads **0-6s**, so **6s remains the observed upper bound** on `trivy fs --download-db-only` with GitHub's cache layer contributing nothing, and the happy path is still bounded at single-digit seconds against 180s. **That qualifier expired at M5.0 and the seventeen samples are now a closed set.** The action upgrades fixed the cache failures that G3 predicted they would not, so from run `32739032131` onward `actions/cache` restores and saves successfully — which means a future reading of this step is no longer measuring a download with nothing cached, and **cannot be added to the seventeen**. The first post-upgrade run reads 0s, and **that reading is not a sample either**: whether it is a cache hit or a fast download is not decidable from the jobs API, which exposes step timings but not logs, so it measures the two possibilities indistinguishably. Practically this weakens the proxy rather than the entry: the bound was always the *upper* end, 6s of it survives from the pre-M5.0 samples, and the number that would actually settle G4 is still a refresh inside a real `trivy fs` on a production worker. What is gone is any claim to know which readings are the real ones. **This strengthens the entry rather than weakening it:** a proxy that varies 0-6s across runs with an identical workflow is one more reason the only number that settles this is a refresh inside a real `trivy fs` on a production worker. **`Confirmed:` deliberately lists two, not three.** M3.7 encountered the *number*; it never encountered the *deferral* — ADR-016 mentions ADR-012, `skip_db_update` and a DB refresh nowhere at all. This register counts a milestone where a gap was re-encountered *and deferred again*, which presupposes somebody read it and chose not to act, and the threshold exists because "the repetition had become information nobody was acting on". An inadvertent carry-forward is arguably worse, but it is a different thing: counting it would make `Confirmed:` mean something in G4 that it does not mean in G1-G3, and would make the field nearly unfalsifiable, since almost any later decision using a deferred value carries it forward. The mechanism for catching *that* failure is review step 5's widened scope, not this field. Treat the entry as though it were at the threshold anyway — it carries a `Deferral rationale:` voluntarily, which is exactly what the threshold would compel. Recorded here rather than only in ADR-012's Consequences because prose has no `Blocks-if-unresolved:` field and no escalation check — the lesson G1 and G2 already record, this time about an ADR.

### G5 — `Finding` has one `scan_id`, but dedup is required *across* scans
Confirmed: M4.0 · Status: resolved → M4.2
Resolution: M4.2 — ADR-0019. The sighting model this entry named: `Finding` is durable and project-scoped, identified by a `dedup_hash` property, with `FindingSighting` recording each scan's observation. Two milestones from confirmation to resolution, and nothing ever reached disk in the wrong shape — the sequencing the Note below defends held. **`Blocks-if-unresolved:` understated it in one way worth recording**, the same way G1's did: the missing concept was not only the sighting but the *scope*. Findings dedup within a project, and `project_id` reaches `normalization` through none of its inputs — `get_succeeded_by_scan_id` returns `ScanResult` rows and `normalization_runs` had no project either — so resolving this also required deciding that the handoff row carries the dedup scope. A read port back into `scanning` cannot serve it, because ADR-0017 decision 2 fixes the reconciliation sweep as selecting on `normalization_runs` alone. That column, its migration and the port signature are M4.3's acceptance criteria.
Deferral rationale: the decision belongs to M4.1/M4.2, which own the `Finding` entity and the dedup strategy respectively — M4.0's scope is explicitly the pipeline boundary, and deciding `Finding`'s shape here would design the entity one issue before the issue that builds it, without a single captured scanner fixture to design against (M4.1's own prerequisite). Recorded now rather than at M4.1 because M4.0 edited `ARCHITECTURE.md` §4 and would otherwise have left the tension sitting in prose it had just touched. Resolve at **M4.2**, the issue whose acceptance criterion is "re-running a scan doesn't duplicate identical findings" — that criterion cannot be met without answering this.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M9.1's re-scan diffing, and M4.2's own acceptance criterion.** `ARCHITECTURE.md` §4 gives `Finding` a single `scan_id` plus a `dedup_hash`, while `PRODUCT_SPEC.md` FR-5 requires deduplication "across repeated scans". With one `scan_id` there are only two outcomes and both are wrong: either the same underlying finding gets a fresh row per scan, so it is duplicated and `dedup_hash` never actually dedupes anything across scans; or the first row is kept and its `scan_id` points at a stale scan, so nothing records that the finding was seen again. M9.1 ("previously open Risks are re-evaluated; resolved ones are marked with the evidence diff that justified it") then cannot distinguish "still present in the latest scan" from "last seen three scans ago" — and it fails *silently*, marking a live finding resolved because no new row carries it. The missing concept is a sighting/occurrence: a `Finding` identified by its dedup hash, with per-scan observations hanging off it.
Note: this is a schema-shape gap, not a bug in anything shipped — no `Finding` code exists yet, which is exactly why it is cheap now and expensive after M4.3 persists the wrong shape and M5 correlates on it.
Note (M4.1): **`Finding` now exists and this entry is still correctly assigned to M4.2 — the sequencing is what makes that safe, and it is written here so a later reader does not mistake it for an oversight.** M4.1 shipped the entity, so at a glance a shape decision now lands *after* the thing it shapes. But M4.1's renumbering put M4.2 ahead of M4.3, so the identity decision still lands before anything is persisted, and the Note above is satisfied on its own terms: nothing reaches disk in the wrong shape, and M5 has nothing to correlate on yet. Moving the entry to M4.1 was considered and rejected — M4.1 had to capture the fixtures first, which is what dissolves this entry's stated blocker, and deciding identity in the same issue would have meant deciding it in the hours after first seeing the data. M4.1 hands M4.2 the per-tool identity candidates instead, including the finding that Semgrep's `fingerprint` is unusable (it is the literal string `"requires login"`), and leaves `dedup_hash` off `Finding` entirely rather than shipping a placeholder.

### G6 — Semgrep's pinned ruleset declares no CWE or OWASP metadata
Confirmed: M4.1, post-M4, M5.1, M6.1 · Status: **open — the M5.1 assignment is discharged, the gap is not; see Discharge (M5.1) below**
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Assignment (post-M4 boundary review): **this entry's own trigger has fired.** The Deferral rationale below says "Re-read when M5.1 picks its matching signals", and M5.1 is the issue now starting — its stated job *is* picking matching signals, and CWE-based matching is one of the two the roadmap names. That is the background-debt-to-critical-path transition review step 3 exists to catch, so it moves from a re-read to an assignment. What M5.1 owes is a decision, not necessarily a fix: resolve G6, or choose signals that do not depend on CWE. Either is fine. Silently choosing CWE matching is not, because it would make M5.3's regression suite — this roadmap's explicit proof artifact — demonstrate correlation over Trivy↔ZAP only, with nothing in the result saying a third of the tools never participated.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M6, silently, and M5's CWE-based matching with it.** `rulesets/default.yml` has no `metadata:` block at all — verified against real captured output, where `extra.metadata` is `{}` — so every Semgrep `Finding` in production carries `cwe = None` and `owasp_category = None`. M6's `RiskReasoning` and any CWE-based correlation in M5 therefore see nothing from SAST: not an error, not a gap anything reports, just an entire signal dimension permanently absent for one of three tools while the code reading it looks correct. Trivy supplies `CweIDs` and ZAP supplies `cweid`, so the asymmetry is invisible unless someone groups findings by `source`. The same empty `metadata` also means Semgrep supplies no confidence, which is one of the inputs M6.1 needs for its own deferred decision on that field. Distinct from **G7**, which is the token-gated half of Semgrep's degraded output and has an independent cause and fix.
Deferral rationale: the two fixes are a production scanning-configuration change, not a mapper change, and neither belongs in an issue about the domain model. Either add `metadata: {cwe: [...], owasp: [...]}` to the pinned rule — cheap, but it is editing what production scans with — or widen the ruleset, which the determinism-over-registry-breadth reasoning in `default.yml`'s own comment argues against without its own decision. The mapper is already correct: it reads both fields when a rule declares them, and a synthetic fixture proves it, so resolving this needs no code in `normalization` at all. Re-read when M5.1 picks its matching signals, and again when M6.1 defines `severity_signal`.
Note: not a bug in anything shipped, and deliberately not worked around — a mapper that invented a CWE would be feeding the Risk Engine an untraceable input, which is rule 5 violated one layer upstream of where anyone would look for it (ADR-0018 decision 4).
Note: this is the *ruleset* half of Semgrep's degraded output. The token half is **G7**, and the two are independent — adding `metadata:` to `default.yml` does nothing for `fingerprint`/`lines`, and setting a token does nothing for CWE or OWASP. Conflating them would make either fix look like both.
Note (M5.1): **M5.1's first commit does not discharge this, and the reason is G23 rather than neglect.** Discharging G6 means deciding whether CWE is a matching signal — which is what its assignment asks for — and **G23 makes that decision unvalidatable today**: the three committed fixtures were captured against three unrelated targets, so no measurement over them can tell "CWE does not correlate these tools" from "these captures describe different systems". ADR-0023 therefore decides the *mechanism* (a match key `correlation` owns) and explicitly defers *which fields the key carries*. Recorded here because without it M5.1 could close with this entry still `assigned → M5.1` and silently undischarged, which is the failure the `Confirmed:`/escalation machinery exists to prevent one field over. ***(Superseded 2026-08-25 by the `Discharge (M5.1)` field below, and the "today" in this paragraph should be read as scoped to the commit its header names. G23 is resolved, the corpus exists, and the decision this paragraph calls unvalidatable has been taken — including for the two bullets in the list below whose headings now begin `~~NOT settled~~ SETTLED at M5.1, empty`. Left in place rather than deleted because it is the record of why the first commit did not discharge the entry, which is the thing this register keeps.)***
What the M5.1 measurement **did** settle, separated from what it did not, because the two rest on different evidence and **only one of the two bases survives G23**:
- **Structural, survives any corpus — `rule_id` can never match cross-tool.** It is each tool's own identifier, namespaced by that tool: `dangerous-eval` (Semgrep's `check_id`), `CVE-2019-11324` (Trivy's `VulnerabilityID`), `10038-1` (ZAP's `alertRef`). Two tools naming the same underlying issue produce different strings by construction, so equality on `rule_id` is a within-tool signal permanently. That is a property of the identifier vocabularies, not of any capture.
- **Structural, survives any corpus — but for ZAP only: no location signal can reach DAST.** The basis is the field *population* pattern, not any pair count. ZAP populates no file or package fields at all (`file_path`, `package`, `installed_version` all `None` on 11 of 11 findings), while Semgrep and Trivy populate no URL fields (`url`, `http_method`, `parameter` all `None` on 1 of 1 and 12 of 12). Two tools that never populate the same field cannot be equal on it regardless of what they scanned, and this is enforced in the mappers rather than accidental: `zap.py` sets no file fields, `semgrep.py` and `trivy.py` set no URL fields. **Deliberately *not* argued from the cross-tool zero-pair counts**, which G23 has just established measure three unrelated captures and therefore cannot carry a structural claim.
  - ***(Counts updated 2026-08-25 against G23's corpus: the population figures above are the pre-G23 ones — now `None` on **13 of 13** ZAP findings, and on **1 of 1 and 20 of 20** for Semgrep and Trivy. **The numbers moved and the property held**, which is the outcome a structural claim should have when its corpus is replaced, and is recorded rather than silently rewritten for the reason G20 exists.)***
  - ***(Corrected 2026-08-24, same day, by the guardian pass on this commit. This bullet first said location-field equality is not a cross-tool signal **at all**, which is false and false in the direction this entry is about. The `Location(...)` constructions in `mappers/semgrep.py` and `mappers/trivy.py` both set `file_path` unconditionally when the tool supplies one — 1 of 1 and 12 of 12 non-`None` — so **Semgrep and Trivy do share a populated location field**. The population evidence quoted above only ever concerned ZAP against the other two; generalising it to all three pairs was an inference, not a measurement, in a bullet headed "survives any corpus". Rewritten rather than annotated.)***
    - ***(Citation form corrected 2026-08-25, and the case is worth keeping because it is a measured instance of what CLAUDE.md's line-number rule forbids. This parenthetical cited `semgrep.py:118` and `trivy.py:106`. Both were correct when written; **`trivy.py:106` had rotted onto a comment line** by the time it was next read, the `file_path=` assignment having moved to 110. The cause is in git: `6d2503c` edited that file's docstring, `+4` net lines entirely above line 106 — the exact displacement — **two commits after the citation was written**, and touching nothing either citation was about. `semgrep.py` was never touched, which is why its number survived. Now cited by symbol. The counts `1 of 1 and 12 of 12` are left as written because they measured the pre-G23 corpus this parenthetical describes; current figures are in the annotation above.)***
- **~~NOT settled~~ SETTLED at M5.1, empty — Semgrep↔Trivy on `file_path`.** Both populate it, so equality is expressible and the question is real. *(This bullet was written against the pre-G23 corpus, where the values were `vulnerable.py` against `requirements.txt` — two unrelated targets, and therefore inadmissible. **G23's corpus makes it admissible and the answer is the same: `app.py` against `requirements.txt`, intersection empty.** The *semantic* argument this bullet offered — that Trivy's is a manifest path and Semgrep's a source path, so they would rarely coincide even on one repo — is now measured rather than argued, and in a sharper form than it was put: Semgrep's `paths.scanned` is `['app.py']`, so it never read the manifest at all, and Trivy's `Target` is the manifest and never a source file. The two vocabularies cannot overlap on this repository. What this does **not** establish is that they never could — a repository where Trivy scans a Dockerfile or a container image has a different `Target` vocabulary, so this stays corpus-dependent rather than being promoted to structural.)*
- **~~NOT settled~~ SETTLED at M5.1, empty — the Trivy↔ZAP CWE zero.** Against the pre-G23 corpus Trivy's CWEs were `{CWE-74, 93, 200, 295, 409, 601, 669, 770}` and ZAP's `{CWE-497, 693, 1021}`, disjoint — a fact about two captures of two unrelated targets, and therefore inadmissible as evidence about the signal. *(Those sets are the pre-G23 corpus's. **Against G23's corpus they are Trivy's 14 and ZAP's 4, still disjoint**, with Semgrep contributing none at all. The caveat this bullet raised is discharged: the zero is now a fact about one artifact scanned three ways rather than about two unrelated captures, so it is admissible. The corpus's own limit survives and is recorded in `tests/fixtures/scanners/README.md` — 14 against 4, over one small Flask app with three vulnerable packages, cannot separate "these families never overlap" from "this one happened not to". ADR-0023 Decision B takes the decision this bullet was waiting for, and cites this cell as one of four grounds rather than as the whole case.)* ***(Superseded 2026-08-25: **this cell is no longer a ground of Decision B, is no longer a zero, and "four grounds" is no longer the arity** — Decision B now rests on **three**, two of the original four having been struck as falsified. Under an active scan the Trivy↔ZAP intersection is `{CWE-22}`. Decision B was re-founded on what that one pair turns out to be — a false match — rather than on the cell's emptiness, so the sentence above is right about the pre-probe corpus and wrong about the decision. See ADR-0023 Decision B as amended.)***

Discharge (M5.1): **what is discharged is the ASSIGNMENT, not the gap, and the distinction is load-bearing rather than bookkeeping.** The assignment above asked for *"a decision, not necessarily a fix: resolve G6, or choose signals that do not depend on CWE. Either is fine."* ADR-0023's amendment takes the second: **Decision B puts `cwe` out of the correlation match key**, citing this entry as one of its grounds. *(This clause read "one of its **four** grounds — *not* independent ones, as the third bullet below sets out". Both halves were superseded 2026-08-25: Decision B now rests on **three** grounds, and the non-independence this clause flagged is **gone** — see the amendment on that third bullet. G6 is still one of the grounds, which is the only part this sentence needed.)* So the decision M5.1 owed has been taken, and this entry no longer sits on M5's critical path. Three things must stay explicit, because an entry reading as *resolved* would break the decision that depends on it:
- **The gap is not resolved.** `rulesets/default.yml` still declares no `metadata:`, every Semgrep `Finding` in production still carries `cwe = None` and `owasp_category = None`, and the `Blocks-if-unresolved:` above still names **M6** — where `RiskReasoning` reads CWE as a scoring input and would see nothing from SAST, silently, exactly as that field describes. Only the M5 half of the blocking claim has been retired.
- **Decision B is conditional on this entry remaining unresolved.** Its **second**-named ground is that Semgrep contributes zero CWEs, which is this gap. So this entry being open is not debt awaiting a tidy-up; it is **the standing precondition of a decision recorded in another document**, and marking it resolved without re-reading ADR-0023 would leave that decision resting on a premise nothing states any more.
- **Resolving it is a trigger to RE-READ Decision B, not to reverse it.** Decision B rests on three grounds: (1) the only cross-tool CWE pair ever measured is a **false match** — ZAP `6-5` at confidence 1 against Trivy `CVE-2024-49766` — (2) this gap, and (3) **G26**'s unverified ordering stability. Resolving G6 removes (2) and **touches nothing else**: ground (1) is a Trivy↔ZAP finding that Semgrep is not party to, and ground (3) is independent of it. That leaves two grounds standing, so closing G6 does not put CWE back in the key; it obliges the re-read. *(Rewritten 2026-08-25 by the active-scan probe. This bullet enumerated **four** grounds and argued that G6 "reaches further into that decision than a count of grounds suggests" because resolving it would reopen two of ground (1)'s three pairs. Two of those four grounds are now struck as falsified and the entanglement is gone, so the enumeration is replaced rather than annotated — a falsified ground that stays on the page is a ground somebody will cite. See ADR-0023's Decision B as amended.)***
Note (post-M4): **this entry's own cheap fix fires ADR-0018's multi-CWE trigger, and the guard that would catch it covers the wrong tool.** Found by the M4→M5 boundary review's step 5 and recorded here because it changes what "cheap" means. ADR-0018 decision 4 makes `cwe` a single `str | None` on the measured basis that the maximum across all three real fixtures is 1, and explicitly leaves `cwes: tuple[str, ...]` as "the right answer if the measurement changes", relying on a fixture guard to fire when it does. That guard is `test_the_real_cwe_cardinality_is_one_apart_from_a_single_two` (renamed at M5.1 from `test_every_real_vulnerability_carries_exactly_one_cwe`, when it fired and the measurement changed) and it lives in `tests/unit/test_trivy_mapper.py` — **Trivy only**. Meanwhile `semgrep.py`'s mapper reads `canonical_cwe(_first(metadata.get("cwe")))`, and Semgrep's `metadata.cwe` is a **list**. So the first option this entry's rationale names — adding `metadata: {cwe: [...], owasp: [...]}` to the pinned rule, "cheap, but it is editing what production scans with" — can hand SAST findings several CWEs of which only the first survives, silently, with nothing failing. ADR-0018 already states the cost of that shape: taking the first "would manufacture silent false negatives in M5 whenever the shared CWE was not first" — which lands precisely in the milestone this entry is now assigned to. *(Updated 2026-08-25: the M5.1 assignment is discharged and `cwe` is out of the match key, so **the M5 half of this hazard is currently inert** — a silent false negative needs a consumer, and correlation is no longer one. It is inert rather than gone, and in a way worth naming: **resolving G6 is exactly what would reactivate it**, since that is also the trigger to re-read ADR-0023 Decision B. The two fire together, which is the opposite of reassuring. M6 reads `cwe` regardless and is unaffected by any of this.)* Whoever closes G6 by editing the ruleset must decide `cwes` at the same time, or add the Semgrep-side cardinality guard the Trivy one implies. The second option — widening the ruleset — has the same property and more of it.

Note (2026-09-16, M6.1): **the M6 half of `Blocks-if-unresolved:` is RETIRED, and the entry stays open.** That field says M6's `RiskReasoning` would read CWE as a scoring input and see nothing from SAST, silently. ADR-0005 decision 4 declines CWE as a scoring input outright — on this gap as one ground, plus `cwe` sitting in `_REFRESHED_COLUMNS` (**G26**) — so the silent-absence failure this entry forecast for M6 **cannot occur**: there is no consumer in `risk_engine` to be silent about. What is left of the blocking claim is the findings API, which returns `cwe` per finding, and **ADR-0023 Decision B, whose second ground is this gap and which is the reason the entry must not be marked resolved**. Both halves of the M5 clause and now the M6 clause are retired; the gap itself — `rulesets/default.yml` declaring no `metadata:` — is untouched, and closing it still obliges the Decision B re-read this entry already specifies.

Note (2026-09-19, post-M7 boundary review) · Confirms: none: **deferred under Rules for M8 rule 1.** M8 ships product only, and this entry does not trace to M8's exit condition. Trigger: **the M8→M9 boundary review**.

### G7 — Two Semgrep fields are redacted because no `SEMGREP_APP_TOKEN` is set, and setting one silently arms a rule-12 exposure
Confirmed: M4.1, M4.5, M7.3 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **`GET /projects/{project_id}/findings/{finding_id}/evidence` (`GetFindingEvidenceUseCase`) and M4.2's identity precision, in opposite directions, on a config change nobody would connect to either.** Semgrep OSS redacts `extra.fingerprint` and `extra.lines` to the literal string `"requires login"` for anonymous users, and this repo sets `SEMGREP_APP_TOKEN` nowhere — not in `ci.yml`, not in `Settings`, not in `.env`. Verified against real captured output. Two consequences, both of which flip the moment somebody adds a token for better rules or telemetry: (1) **`extra.lines` becomes the matched source line.** It is inside `Evidence.raw_payload`, which the evidence route named above returns verbatim — since M4.5 that is a concrete path, not a future one, and it is the ONLY route in the system that returns scanned content (the listing route deliberately returns evidence metadata only). Secret-detection rules match secrets — so a token turns a rule-12-inert field into a rule-12-live one with no code change and no review of M4.5. (2) **`extra.lines` also becomes the discriminator M4.2's identity model does not have.** Sharpened by ADR-0019, which declined tool-supplied fingerprints on their own grounds (per-tool by construction, and Trivy's is opaque) but *did* exclude `start_line`/`end_line` from `dedup_hash`, accepting that two matches of one rule in one file collapse into one `Finding`. A hash of the matched source line is the only thing that would keep them distinct without putting line numbers back into the identity, and `lines` is where it would come from. So the same token that arms the leak in (1) would improve identity in (2) — and changing the hash inputs is a `DEDUP_HASH_VERSION` bump plus a re-normalization, not a free edit.
Deferral rationale: nothing is wrong today — no token means no source in the payload — so there is nothing to fix, only something to *notice at the right moment*, which is exactly what this register is for and what prose has repeatedly failed to do (the G1 lesson). Adding a token is a real decision with its own trade-offs (Semgrep's registry becomes a network dependency of every scan, which `rulesets/default.yml`'s own comment argues against, and it sends scan telemetry off-box) and it is not M4.1's to make. M4.2 picked its identity inputs without one and recorded what that costs (ADR-0019 decision 3); re-read at M4.5 before deciding what the endpoint exposes, and again whenever a token is proposed — at which point both halves above fire together.
Note: the fix, if a token is ever added, is not to remove it: it is to decide deliberately what M4.5 returns, per rule 12 and rule 10 (a dedicated response schema, never the entity). Separately from `Finding`, this is also why M4.1 did *not* treat `"requires login"` as a sentinel and null it out — the string is the tool's real output, and `raw_payload` is a verbatim copy by design (ADR-0018 decision 6).
Note (M4.5): **the thing that would leak now exists and has a path, so this entry names it instead of a milestone.** `GET /projects/{project_id}/findings/{finding_id}/evidence` (`GetFindingEvidenceUseCase`) returns `Evidence.raw_payload` verbatim, and that is the only route in the system that returns scanned content. The listing route deliberately does not, so the exposure is one finding per request at a URL of its own rather than every finding in a listing — which bounds it, and makes it auditable and independently rate-limitable at M10.2, but does not remove it. Consequence (1) above is therefore **live the moment a token is set**, with no code change and no review, and the route to re-read is that one. Consequence (2) is unchanged. See ADR-0022 decision 1.

Note (2026-09-17, M7.3 commit 2): **the first prompt carrying scanned content ships, and it cannot arm this entry.** ADR-0034 decision 1 excludes `Evidence.raw_payload` from every prompt, so a `SEMGREP_APP_TOKEN` turning `extra.lines` into a source line would still reach the evidence route and no prompt. `BriefMember` holds only a finding's typed `title` and `Location`. Pinned by `tests/unit/test_generate_security_brief.py::test_no_raw_payload_byte_reaches_the_describe_prompt`, which fills members through the real fill site from the committed active ZAP capture and asserts neither its `<p>` markup nor any of its `solution`'s consecutive 40-character slices, at every offset, is rendered. The evidence route's exposure is unchanged, and so is this entry's status.

Note (2026-09-19, post-M7 boundary review) · Confirms: none: **deferred under Rules for M8 rule 1.** M8 ships product only, and this entry does not trace to M8's exit condition. Trigger: **the M8→M9 boundary review**.

### G8 — `ruff --fix` runs on every commit and can silently rewrite a test into a weaker one
Confirmed: M4.1, M5.0, M5.8, post-M4 · Status: **open — narrowed at M5.0, not closed**
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Note (post-M5.0): **the analysis below was performed against the wrong rule set and is re-derived here; a guard is now in place; the entry stays open and the residual is named.**
- **Re-derived, and the first re-derivation was itself wrong — recorded because that is this entry's own subject.** The counts must come from `ruff check --show-settings`, which reports the **enabled** set resolved from the actual config, composed with fix availability from `ruff rule --all --output-format json`. Over this repo's `select = ["E","F","I","UP","B","SIM","C4"]`: **v0.6.9 enables 186 rules of which 111 are autofixable; 0.16.3 enables 196 of which 122 are.** Thirteen rules become newly enabled (`B039 B911 B912 C420 SIM905 UP042`–`UP047 UP049 UP050`), three stop being enabled (`E999 UP027 UP038`), thirteen codes become newly autofixable — those, **less** `B039` and `B911`, which arrive enabled but carry no fix, **plus** `B028` and **`E402`**, which were already enabled and only gained one — and two lose their fix (`UP027`, `UP038`), for **+11 autofixable on net**.
  - *An earlier version of this bullet said "214 of which 130" and "227 of which 142", from an ad-hoc enumeration, and was wrong twice over.* It **dropped every `C4` rule**, because it derived a prefix by stripping trailing digits — so `C400` became `C`, which never matched the `C4` selector — and it **counted preview rules that cannot fire** with `preview` unset. Audited mechanically afterwards: `C4` is the **only** selector in this repo whose prefix ends in a digit, and all 19 of its enabled rules were dropped while 0 rules were dropped for `E`, `F`, `I`, `UP`, `B` or `SIM`. The same bug returns the moment a selector like `C9` is added. Both errors are the entry's own shape — a count that omits rules which *can* fire and one that includes rules which *cannot* are the same defect in opposite directions.
- **What that changed, beyond the arithmetic.** The specific conclusion still survives — none of the newly-autofixable codes reorders operands, SIM300 is still the operand-order hazard, and `test_severity.py` is still closed by `operator`-function form. But the scope was **not** "understated by twelve incidental autofixes": the family that went missing was **flake8-comprehensions**, 18 autofixable rules that are structural rewrites by definition — `C417` turns `map(f, xs)` into a comprehension, `C416` removes one, `C400`/`C405` rewrite between generators, literals and calls. They meet this entry's written criterion exactly, and the first draft of the guard omitted them only because the enumeration never saw them. **All 18 are now on `unfixable`, which stands at 30 codes.** *(`C415` is enabled but carries no fix, so it is deliberately absent.)*
- **`E402` is a genuinely new hazard of exactly this entry's shape**: its fix relocates an import, and a test that imports late on purpose (after an env var or a monkeypatch) would be rewritten into one that no longer tests that.
- **Bounded, so the guard is not stated wider than it holds.** M5.0 brought 30 Markdown files into the `ruff-format` hook's file set (see G12), and that does **not** extend `--fix` over ADR prose: `ruff check docs/` reports *"No Python files found"* — ruff's linter does not read Markdown even at 0.16.3, only its formatter does. The new surface is formatting, not rewriting.
- **Guarded.** `pyproject.toml`'s `[tool.ruff.lint]` now carries `unfixable` over **30 codes** — the comparison and boolean-collapse rewrites (`SIM300`, `SIM201`/`SIM202`, `SIM220`–`SIM223`, `E711`–`E714`), `E402`'s import relocation, and the 18 autofixable `C4` comprehension rewrites. **This is not a suppression**: the rules still fire and still fail CI, they only lose the ability to rewrite. It is therefore *not* the `# noqa` this entry rejected, and *not* the `per-file-ignores` proposed when it was opened, which would have stopped detection in CI as well as autofix. Verified rather than assumed, with a throwaway probe: `ruff check --fix` over a file with unsorted imports and two yoda conditions sorts the imports and reports "Found 3 errors (1 fixed, 2 remaining)", leaving both comparisons untouched. `I001` is deliberately absent from the list, so import-sort autofix — the daily convenience that dropping `--fix` would have cost — is kept.
- **Landed at M5.0 rather than M5.1 on purpose**: M5.1 writes the correlation-symmetry tests that are this entry's named trigger, and a guard that arrives in the same issue as its trigger is one that depends on somebody reading a roadmap line while writing the tests. This entry is the record of that failing once.
- **What stays open, in these words:** `unfixable` covers the enumerated codes and nothing else. The hook still runs `--fix`, so for any code not on that list it still rewrites the file, fails the commit, and leaves the original staged — the mechanism described below, unchanged. A future ruff release adding a rule that rewrites expression structure, which nobody adds to the list, is still this failure. That is the residual: not "mostly fixed", and not something the suppression metric will ever show, since `check_claims.py` does not parse ruff config at all. Re-read condition is unchanged, plus two: **at any ruff version bump**, check the autofixable-rule delta rather than only the diff — and **at any change to `[tool.ruff.lint]`'s `select` or `preview`**, since these counts are the *enabled* set and turning `preview` on would move every one of them without a version bump. That makes the stated basis self-guarding.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **any future `--fix` over a test whose assertion is structural — the result is a test that passes while asserting less than its name, its docstring and (in the case that produced this entry) its ADR all claim, with nothing in the pipeline that detects the gap.** It has already happened once, in the commit that opened this entry. `test_severity.py` asserted that `Severity`'s comparison operators reject a non-`Severity` operand in **both** operand orders. `ruff`'s SIM300 (yoda-conditions) rewrote `Severity.HIGH < other` to `other > Severity.HIGH` and `Severity.HIGH >= other` to `other <= Severity.HIGH`, which collapsed four distinct assertions into two duplicated pairs and deleted every direct-order case. The rewrite is *semantically* valid — both forms raise — which is exactly why no tool objected: in a test whose subject **is** the operand order, a semantics-preserving rewrite is still a coverage-destroying one, and ruff cannot know the difference. ADR-0018 then stated in writing that both orders were "verified directly, not assumed, and pinned by a test". The verification had been correct; the tool invalidated it afterwards, and the claim outlived it. This is the `test_tampered_state_is_rejected` failure shape with the author replaced by an automatic tool.
The mechanism, verified rather than assumed *(against the artifact as it stood at M4.1; **M5.0 replaced that artifact and this sentence is updated with it** — the config no longer has a hook id `ruff` or an `args:` key at all, and `--fix` now lives in `entry: uv run ruff check --force-exclude --fix`. Behaviour identical, description no longer matching, which is this entry's own failure shape and is why it is corrected here rather than left for a reader to reconcile)*: `.pre-commit-config.yaml` runs its ruff-check hook with `--fix`, so it fires on **every commit**, not only when invoked by hand. `ci.yml` runs `ruff check .` with no `--fix`, so CI never introduces a rewrite — and never catches one either. Probed directly with a throwaway file: the hook rewrites on disk, fails the commit with "files were modified by this hook", and leaves the **original** in the index, so the developer re-runs `git add` and commits. `pytest` is not a pre-commit hook, so the rewritten test is never re-executed before the commit lands; CI then runs the weakened test and it goes green. Nothing else looks: `check_claims.py` re-derives claims from artifacts but cannot see that an assertion stopped asserting what it says, the suppression metric is blind to it, and review step 5 reads ADRs rather than tests.
Deferral rationale: the blast radius today is **one file, and it is closed.** `Severity` is the only type in `src/` that overrides a comparison operator (verified by grep for `__lt__`/`__le__`/`__gt__`/`__ge__`/`__eq__`/`total_ordering`), `test_severity.py` is the only test where operand order is the subject, and it is now written with `operator.lt(a, b)` rather than infix — a function call is not a comparison expression, so SIM300 has nothing to rewrite. `ruff check tests/ --diff` and `ruff check src/ --diff` are both currently empty, and a scan of `tests/` for the other rewritable structural patterns (constant-on-left comparisons, `== None`/`== True`, `not x in`) returns zero hits. So there is nothing to fix right now — only a trap to notice the next time a test's subject is the *shape* of an expression. **Explicitly not resolved with `# noqa: SIM300`:** that would take the tracked suppression count from 0 to 1 for convenience, which is precisely the decay the metric exists to detect, and it would leave the rewrite one deleted comment away from happening again.
Note (post-M4): **this entry's trigger arrives in M5.1, and the entry now says where.** Its re-read condition is "whenever a test is added whose subject is argument order, operand order, or expression structure" — and M5.1's acceptance criteria require "should NOT correlate" cases, which for a matching function means **symmetry**: does A match B iff B matches A. That is operand order, and it is the one subject SIM300 rewrites. Option (a) below costs nothing and is already the house style, so M5.1's roadmap entry carries it as an instruction rather than leaving it to be rediscovered. Separately: **G12's assignment to M5.0 partly invalidates this entry's own analysis** — the rule-set reasoning below was performed against the *pinned* hook (v0.6.9), not the ruff CI resolves, so which rules `--fix` can actually rewrite is not settled until that version skew is closed. M5.0 re-reads this entry as part of closing G12. *(Discharged: the skew is closed and the analysis is re-derived in the post-M5.0 Note at the top of this entry. M5.1's instruction stands regardless — `operator`-function form is still the house style, and `unfixable` is a second line of defence rather than a replacement for it.)*
Note (2026-08-25): **the note above is FALSIFIED, not incomplete, and only as to *where*** — "this entry's trigger arrives in M5.1" is a claim about which issue fires the trigger, and it is false. M5.1 closed without writing a correlation test of any kind: `CorrelateFindingsUseCase` and the "should NOT correlate" cases that *are* this entry's trigger were never built, which is registered as **G30**. **The trigger arrives at M5.8**, which carries the `operator`-form instruction as its criterion (c). **Left standing rather than rewritten, and the reason is specific rather than a default:** that note carries a second, independent claim — G12's version skew invalidating this entry's own rule-set analysis, and its discharge — which nothing here touches, and rewriting the opening sentence would take it along. Its closing "M5.1's instruction stands regardless" is true of the instruction and stale as to the number; read it with M5.8 in M5.1's place.
Note (2026-08-26, M5.8): **the trigger the note above redirects to M5.8 has now arrived, and it was met — so what this note records is the arithmetic this entry's own `Deferral rationale:` gets wrong from here on.** That field says the blast radius is *one file, and it is closed*. It is now **two**: `tests/unit/test_severity.py` and `tests/unit/test_match_key.py`, whose "should NOT correlate" cases have operand order as their subject and are written in the required form. **The form is the analogue rather than the copy, and the difference is worth naming so the next reader does not think the instruction was followed loosely.** In `test_severity.py` the subject was the comparison operators themselves, so `operator.lt`/`le`/`gt`/`ge` were literally the functions under test. Here the subject is a two-argument predicate, `matches(a, b)`, which is already a call and therefore already has nothing SIM300 could invert — so the operand-order coverage is carried by two named module-level wrappers, one per direction, parametrized over both. The property both forms buy is identical: **none of the operand-order assertions is written as a comparison expression**, so the coverage cannot be autofixed away. Stated at that width deliberately, because the wider version is false and this entry is the one that exists to catch a claim outliving its verification: `test_match_key.py` contains **eight** `==` expressions, all ordinary assertions on collected results, none carrying operand-order coverage and none with a constant on the left for SIM300 to invert. The guarantee is about which assertions are structural, not about the file being free of comparisons. **The residual below is untouched** — `unfixable` still covers the enumerated codes and nothing else, and a future rule that rewrites expression structure and never gets added to that list is still this failure.
Note: the real options, when this next matters, are (a) keep writing such tests in `operator`-function form, which is what M4.1 did and costs nothing, (b) drop `--fix` from the pre-commit hook so the tool reports and a human edits, or (c) add `pytest` to pre-commit so a rewrite is re-executed before the commit lands — (c) is the only one that generalizes beyond comparisons, and it is also the one that puts the full suite in the commit path, which `CLAUDE.md`'s tracked runtime makes expensive. Re-read whenever a test is added whose subject is argument order, operand order, or expression structure, and at any change to `.pre-commit-config.yaml`.
Note (2026-09-15, M5→M6 boundary): **this entry's re-read condition fired at `25488a6` (M5.9) and was not recorded here** — that commit added `exclude` lines to `.pre-commit-config.yaml`, which G21 recorded. Not re-read at this boundary; recorded so the next change to that file is not taken for the first.

Note (2026-09-19, post-M7 boundary review) · Confirms: none: **deferred under Rules for M8 rule 1.** M8 ships product only, and this entry does not trace to M8's exit condition. Trigger: **the M8→M9 boundary review**.

### G9 — Semgrep reports absolute paths, and production's are unique per scan
Confirmed: M4.2 · Status: **RESOLVED (M4.4)** — `SemgrepAdapter` now runs with `cwd=target` and passes `.`; see G10 for the half that had to be fixed in the same change, and ADR-0019's Amendments for the four-row measurement. The adjacent Trivy question is closed too: `Results[].Target` is rendered relative to the scan root even when the argument is absolute (re-measured against `aquasec/trivy` with a `mkdtemp`-shaped mount; `ArtifactName` echoes the absolute path, `Target` does not, and the mapper reads `Target`), so `TrivyAdapter` needed no change.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M4.2's own acceptance criterion for one tool of three, M5's file-path correlation, and M4.5's response surface — all silently.** `SemgrepAdapter.run()` passes an absolute `target` and `GitRepoCheckout` builds it with `tempfile.mkdtemp(prefix="verion-scan-")`, so `results[].path` carries a different absolute prefix on every scan. `file_path` is a `dedup_hash` input (ADR-0019 decision 3), so **every Semgrep finding re-keys on every scan and nothing dedupes** — the exact outcome M4.2 exists to prevent, for SAST only, while Trivy and ZAP dedup correctly. M5 then compares a SAST path that can never match anything, and M4.5 returns `/tmp/verion-scan-<random>/…` worker paths in a response body. Nothing raises in any of the three.
Deferral rationale: the fix is in `scanning`'s adapter — run with `cwd=target` and pass `.` — and M4.2's scope is `normalization/domain/`, with no adapter and no other module touched. Assigned to **M4.4**, the issue where the pipeline first runs end to end and therefore the first place a real Semgrep path reaches a mapper. Nothing is broken in the meantime, because no production path normalizes anything until M4.4 lands.
Note (M4.3): **the fix named above is not sufficient on its own — see G10.** Running with `cwd=target` puts the ruleset outside the working directory, which changes Semgrep's `check_id` and therefore `rule_id`, a second `dedup_hash` input. Whoever does this work must settle both in the same change, or fixing `file_path` will re-key every Semgrep finding through the other field.
Note: **verified by measurement, twice, not deduced.** Against the pinned ruleset with an absolute target, `paths.scanned[]` is `['C:\…\src\verion\platform\__init__.py', …]`; run with `cwd` set to that directory and `.` as the argument, it is `['__init__.py', 'app.py', …]`. The same measurement shows why nothing caught it: `tests/fixtures/scanners/README.md` records that the captured `results[].path` was **redacted** from the absolute capture path to `vulnerable.py`, so every mapper test exercises the shape the fix will produce while production emits the shape it will not. That is G8's shape with a redaction in the tool's place — a verification that is sound and disconnected from reality — and it is why this entry says *measured* rather than *expected*. When the fix lands the fixture becomes accurate rather than needing a re-capture. One adjacent check M4.4 should make while it is there, which one capture could not settle: `trivy fs` was only ever captured inside a container mount, so whether it keeps `Results[].Target` relative against an absolute host path is unverified — same failure if it does not, same fix.

### G10 — Semgrep's `check_id` is the ruleset path *relative to the worker's CWD*, so `rule_id` is not stable
Confirmed: M4.3 · Status: **RESOLVED (M4.4)** — by `--no-rewrite-rule-ids`, semgrep's own documented switch, which makes `check_id` the rule's own `id` and therefore independent of CWD, install path and container `WORKDIR`. **This entry's own proposed fix — pinning the ruleset path in the container image — was dropped rather than adopted**: it makes the value stable within one deployment and different between a developer's machine, CI and production, so the two contexts that verify the code would produce a `rule_id` production never writes, which is the disconnected verification G8 and G9 both record. It also left an absolute worker filesystem path inside a field M4.5 returns. Measurement table in ADR-0019's Amendments.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **the same dedup criterion G9 names, through a second field, and G9's own fix is what fires it.** `rule_id` is a `dedup_hash` input (ADR-0019 decision 3) and Semgrep derives `check_id` from the `--config` path rendered *relative to the process's current working directory* — not, as ADR-0019 decision 2 recorded, merely from where the file sits. `Settings.semgrep_ruleset` is already an absolute path, so the value depends on where the worker runs and where the package is installed: it differs between a developer's machine, CI and a container, and **no fixture-based test can ever observe production's**. Three consequences after M4.3 persists findings: (1) **G9's prescribed fix re-keys every stored Semgrep finding** — running with `cwd=target` over a `tempfile.mkdtemp` puts the ruleset outside the working directory and flips `check_id` to the absolute dotted install path, so fixing `file_path` breaks `rule_id` in the same change; (2) any redeploy to a different install path, or a container `WORKDIR` change, does the same, producing duplicate rows and the fabricated resolutions in M9.1 that ADR-0019's own principle exists to prevent; (3) `rule_id` is a `Finding` field, so M4.5 returns an absolute worker filesystem path in a response body — the disclosure G9 names for `file_path`, arriving on a second field. Nothing raises in any of the three.
Deferral rationale: the fix is in `scanning`'s adapter or in deployment configuration, and M4.3's scope is `normalization`'s persistence. Assigned to **M4.4** because M4.4 owns the G9 adapter change that triggers this and is the first issue with a production writer — shipping that invocation change without deciding what `rule_id` should be would be perverse. Nothing is broken in the meantime: no production path writes a finding until M4.4. **The obvious mitigation is wrong and is ruled out here so it is not re-proposed:** normalizing `check_id` to its last dotted segment in the mapper collapses registry namespaces (`python.lang.security.audit.dangerous-eval` and any other ruleset's `…dangerous-eval` become one rule), trading a rare, loud, deliberate trigger for a permanent silent collision — ADR-0019 decision 3's principle inverted, since it *fabricates* merges rather than under-counting. Pinning the ruleset to a fixed absolute path in the container image is the plausible real fix, and it is a deployment decision.
Note: **measured across three working directories, not deduced.** With one identical absolute `--config` and one identical target file, `check_id` is `src.verion.modules.scanning.adapters.outbound.scanners.rulesets.dangerous-eval` from the repo root, `verion.modules.…rulesets.dangerous-eval` from `<repo>/src`, and `C.Users.…Verion.src.verion.modules.…rulesets.dangerous-eval` from a directory outside the repo. The committed fixture carries the first of those because the capture ran under pytest from the repo root; `check_id` was not among the redacted fields, so the fixture is accurate about what that invocation produces and silent about what any other does.
Note: distinct from **G9**, which is about `results[].path` and `file_path`. Same adapter, same issue, same measurement session — but a different field, a different `Blocks-if-unresolved:`, and G9's fix is this entry's *trigger* rather than its remedy, so folding the two together would blur the fix with a consequence of the fix.

### G11 — Three tables now depend on `scans` and `projects` never being deleted, and no foreign key says so
Confirmed: M4.3, M5.2, M7.2 · Status: assigned → M8.1
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **any future retention or archival policy on `scans`/`scan_results`, silently, in two independent ways.** ADR-0017 decision 1 leaves cross-module references unconstrained, so `findings.project_id`, `evidence.scan_id` and `finding_sightings.scan_id` carry no foreign key — nothing at the database level stops a scan or a project being deleted out from under them. (1) Deleting scans orphans every `finding_sightings` row and leaves `Evidence.scan_id` pointing at nothing, which is the provenance ADR-0019 decision 5 added that field to make checkable; M9.1's absence check would then read a sighting history with holes in it and report resolutions that never happened. (2) Less obviously, it removes **the only migration path for a `DEDUP_HASH_VERSION` bump**: ADR-0019 decision 6 defines that migration as re-normalization from the retained `ScanResult.raw_output`, which works *only* because those rows are keyed `(scan_id, tool)` and never deleted. So a retention policy would quietly make the hash's version prefix unusable for the thing it exists for. Nothing raises in either case.
Deferral rationale: nothing deletes a scan or a project today, and no such policy is proposed — so there is nothing to fix, only something to notice at the right moment, which is what this register is for. Adding `ON DELETE CASCADE` now would not help either: it would decide M9's retention semantics ahead of M9 and would *hide* case (2) rather than surface it, by making the deletion succeed cleanly. Re-read whenever data retention, archival, or a "delete a project" feature is proposed, and at M9.1.
Note (2026-08-26, M5.2): **the forecast below did not land, and the count is still three.** ADR-0025 decision 1 makes a candidate Risk a projection rather than a stored entity, so M5.2 shipped no `risks` table and no link table. The forecast is not withdrawn — it moves to **M8.1**, the first issue forced to persist a Risk, and M6.3 if it takes the optional early write. The note below is left as written rather than corrected: it was accurate about the design M5.2 was expected to have, and what changed is the design, not the reasoning.
Note (post-M4): **the count grows again at M5.2, and the growth is the thing this entry tracks.** A `Risk` correlates `Finding`s across a module boundary, so its link table references `findings.id` from `correlation` — cross-module, therefore no foreign key by ADR-0017 decision 1's rule, therefore a **fourth** table depending on rows never being deleted. Recorded as a forecast rather than a `Confirmed:` entry, since M5.2 has not been built and this register counts milestones where a gap was re-encountered *and deferred again*. What it means for the M9.1 re-read below: by then the question is not "should three tables have FKs" but whether an entire second module's data hangs off the same unconstrained assumption.
Note: half of this is already stated in `dedup.py`'s own comment and in ADR-0019 decision 6, which is exactly why it is registered — prose has no `Blocks-if-unresolved:` field and no escalation check, the lesson G1, G2 and G4 each record. What M4.3 changes is the count: before this issue the dependency was one comment about a hypothetical migration; now three tables hold real rows that depend on it.
Note (2026-09-16, M6→M7 boundary): **That M8.1 is the first issue forced to persist a Risk is QUALIFIED, not struck.** M7.2 persists a `SecurityBrief` about a Risk and may be forced to store the Risk to key it, depending on a decision it has not taken (**G68**). If it is, this entry's fourth-table forecast moves to M7.2.
Note (2026-09-17, M7.2 commit 1): **the qualification above is discharged: M7.2 writes no Risk row.** ADR-0033 decision 1 gives a Brief its own id and holds its Risk's finding-id set as data, so the `Risk`→`Finding` link forecast stays at M8.1. Whether the table the implementation commit adds is a new dependency of this entry's kind is a separate question, and it is recorded here when that commit lands.
Note (2026-09-17, M7.2 commit 2): **`security_briefs` is a fourth table depending on rows never being deleted, and it depends on two of them.**
- **`project_id`** is cross-module and carries no foreign key, as `findings.project_id` does not.
- **`finding_ids` is `ARRAY(String)`**, and an array cannot carry a foreign key at all.
- **Why it matters.** Deleting a finding leaves a stored Brief whose FR-9 link names a finding the evidence route no longer returns. Deleting a project orphans its Briefs.
- **Where it sits in this entry.** Nothing deletes either today, so the entry's deferral rationale holds unchanged. The count is four tables, and a retention or "delete a project" proposal now has Briefs to account for too.
- **Unchanged.** The `Risk`→`Finding` link forecast stays at M8.1, because M7.2 wrote no Risk row.

Assignment (2026-09-19, post-M7 boundary review) · Confirms: none: **assigned to M8.1**, whose text already carries this entry's fourth table.

### G12 — `pre-commit` and CI run different versions of ruff, so a green hook does not mean a green build
Confirmed: M4.3, post-M4 · Status: **resolved → M5.0**
Resolution: M5.0 — the `ruff-pre-commit` repo is gone, replaced by two `local` hooks invoking `uv run ruff check --force-exclude --fix` and `uv run ruff format --force-exclude`. The second of the two options below, because it is the one that cannot drift: there is no second pin to keep in step, the version is whatever `uv.lock` resolves, and that is the same binary `ci.yml` runs.
**The recorded mechanism was wrong, and measuring it is what settled the choice between the two options.** This entry attributed the episode to the versions being "ten minor versions apart, with rule additions, formatter changes and default changes in between". Measured at M5.0 by running both binaries over the repo root: **it is a file-set difference, not a rule-set one.** `ruff format --check .` reports **278 files** under v0.6.9 and **308** under 0.16.3, and the repo tracks exactly 278 `.py` files and exactly 30 `.md` files. 0.16.3's *formatter* discovers Markdown and formats the Python code blocks inside it; 0.6.9's does not — handed a `.md` explicitly it fails to parse it as Python. That is precisely and only why a Python block inside `docs/adr/0020-…md` passed the hook and failed CI. Both trees were otherwise clean under both versions (`check .`, `check --diff .`, and 0.16.3's `--diff --unsafe-fixes .`, all empty), so the ten-version gap contributed nothing to the observed failure.
**The consequence is that option 1 would not have closed it.** `ruff-pre-commit`'s own `.pre-commit-hooks.yaml` **at v0.16.3** still declares `types_or: [python, pyi, jupyter]`, so bumping the `rev` aligns the version and leaves the file set wrong — the same ADR block would still slip past the hook and still be caught only by CI. The `local` hook wins on a second, independent ground the entry did not know about: its selectors are ours, and `ruff-format`'s carries `markdown` deliberately. `ruff-check`'s does not, and that asymmetry is real rather than an oversight — `ruff check docs/` reports *"No Python files found"*: the linter does not read Markdown even at 0.16.3, only the formatter does.
Two costs, recorded because they are the price of `language: system` rather than a pre-commit-managed env. **The first was recorded wrongly and is corrected here by measurement, in the commit that made the claim:** it said a `git commit` in a fresh clone before `uv sync` "now fails in the hook", called that acceptable because it "fails loudly and early", and was false. Tested on a real fresh clone with no `.venv`, running the hook's own command: `uv run ruff --version` creates the virtualenv, installs 107 packages and exits 0. **`uv run` syncs the project itself**, so the hook succeeds — the actual cost is a *slow first commit*, and a hard failure only if `uv` is missing from `PATH` or the machine is offline with a cold cache. That is a smaller and differently-shaped cost, and it dissolves the argument the original sentence used to justify it. The second cost stands: `pre-commit autoupdate` no longer manages ruff, so **the version trigger moves to `uv.lock`** and a quiet autoupdate is no longer evidence that ruff is current. Both are stated in the config's own comment.
Assignment (post-M4 boundary review): **assigned because M5.0 exists for G3, not because this entry earned an issue of its own — stated plainly so the precedent is not misread.** On its own this stays deferred: its failure is *loud* (a red CI, roughly two minutes, obvious cause), it has been caught once already, and the register exists so that "not now, with a verifiable exit condition" is a complete answer. What makes it ride along is that the work is the same class as G3's — bump, read the diff, verify — over the same two files, so one pass costs less than two, and both entries' rationales already say the work deserves a pass of its own. The one thing that is *not* merely convenience: **G8's analysis is evaluated against the wrong tool until this is fixed**, so M5.0 re-reads G8 as part of closing this.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **any commit whose formatting or lint outcome differs between the two versions — the hook reports green and CI fails, or worse, `--fix` applies a rewrite CI would never have made.** `.pre-commit-config.yaml` pinned `astral-sh/ruff-pre-commit` at **v0.6.9** (until M5.0), which pre-commit installs in its own isolated environment; `pyproject.toml`'s `dev` dependency group asks only for `ruff>=0.6`, so the project environment CI uses resolves to **0.16.3** — ten minor versions apart, with rule additions, formatter changes and default changes in between. It fired in this issue and was caught by review rather than by either tool: a Python code block inside `docs/adr/0020-finding-upsert-semantics.md` was accepted by the hook and rejected by `uv run ruff format --check .` (`ci.yml`'s **`Format check (ruff format)`** step). **Measured directly, not deduced:** with the unformatted block staged, `pre-commit run ruff-format --all-files` printed `Passed` while `uv run ruff format --check .` printed `1 file would be reformatted`, in the same working tree, seconds apart. The second-order cost is that **G8's own mitigation is evaluated against the wrong tool**: that entry reasons about which rules `--fix` can rewrite, and the hook doing the rewriting is not the linter CI runs, so its rule set is not the one that analysis covered.
Deferral rationale: bumping the pin is not a one-line change to make blind — ten versions of `--fix` behaviour is real rewrite surface over a codebase whose tests G8 already records being silently weakened once, and the right move is to bump, run `ruff check --diff` and `ruff format --diff` over both trees, and read what changes before staging anything. That deserves its own pass rather than riding inside an issue about persistence, which is the same reasoning G3 uses for the CI action majors. **Two options when it is done**, and they are not equivalent: pin the hook to the exact version `uv.lock` resolves and keep them in step by hand, or drop the `ruff-pre-commit` repo entirely in favour of a `local` hook invoking `uv run ruff`, which makes one version definitionally true for both. The second is the one that cannot drift again.
Note: this is not the same failure as **G8**, though they share a hook. G8 is about `--fix` rewriting a test into a weaker one that still passes; this is about the hook and CI disagreeing on what the rules even are. Fixing either leaves the other — and that held: **G8 is still open after this was resolved**, narrowed by M5.0's `unfixable` list rather than closed by the alignment. What M5.0 did discharge is this entry's second-order cost: G8's rule-set analysis has now been re-derived against the ruff CI actually runs (**111 → 122** autofixable among the rules that can actually fire), and the result is recorded in G8 itself. Re-read at any change to `.pre-commit-config.yaml`, at any ruff bump, and whenever a lint or format failure appears in CI that did not appear locally.

### G13 — A `run_scan` retry after normalization completed leaves findings derived from superseded scanner output
Confirmed: M4.4 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M9.1's resolution detection, and any M4.5 response that claims to show a scan's findings — silently, with nothing raising.** `NormalizationRunRepositoryPort.request` is `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` and must never `DO UPDATE` (ADR-0017 decision 2: overwriting would reset a running or completed row back to pending and re-normalize a scan that was already normalized). So a retried `run_scan` that re-runs every enabled scanner and upserts new `ScanResult` rows **cannot** move the handoff row back to `pending`. If normalization already reached `completed` — which is terminal (ADR-0021 decision 3), so `claim` refuses it and the sweep does not select it — nothing ever re-normalizes. The stored `Finding` rows and sightings then reflect attempt 1's scanner output while `scan_results` holds attempt 2's, and the two disagree with nothing recording that they do. Narrow, but not hypothetical: it needs `run_scan` to fail *after* the handoff write on the non-DB path, which ADR-0017 decision 2 already documents as a real window.
Deferral rationale: every available fix is a decision larger than this issue. A `DO UPDATE` is forbidden by the ADR and would reintroduce exactly the re-normalization loop it was chosen to prevent. A re-normalization trigger — `run_scan` resetting the row when it *actually* rewrote a `ScanResult` — is new behaviour nobody has scoped, and it needs a way to tell "this retry changed the output" from "this retry produced the same output", which `ScanResult`'s upsert currently does not report. Nothing is broken today because no production deployment exists to retry anything. Re-read at **M9.1**, and at any work on re-normalization or on `ScanResult` retention.
Note: this is a *consequence* of two correct decisions meeting, not a defect in either. ADR-0017 decision 2 is right that `DO UPDATE` is wrong, and ADR-0021 decision 3 is right that `completed` is terminal; the gap is that neither anticipated the input changing underneath a finished stage. Registered rather than left in ADR-0021's Consequences for the reason G1, G2 and G4 each record: prose has no `Blocks-if-unresolved:` field and no escalation check.

### G14 — A sighting can outlive the tool result that produced it
Confirmed: M4.4 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M9.1's absence check, through the one caveat ADR-0019 already names — made reachable by a retry rather than by a partial scan.** ADR-0019's Consequences records that "not sighted in the latest scan" only means resolved *for the tools that succeeded in that scan*, and requires M9.1 to scope its absence check by `get_succeeded_by_scan_id`. G14 is the sharper form: scan N's Semgrep sightings are already persisted, then a `run_scan` retry turns that tool's `ScanResult` from `SUCCEEDED` to `FAILED` (ADR-016 decision 1's retry property, which that ADR explicitly does not close). "Sighted in scan N" is now true for a tool that, as the database currently records it, failed in scan N. A scoped absence check reads a sighting history that is not merely incomplete but *inconsistent with its own provenance*, and the inconsistency is invisible: `finding_sightings` carries no tool, by design, since `source` is a `dedup_hash` input and no `(finding_id, scan_id)` can span two tools.
Deferral rationale: same window as **G13** and probably the same fix — both need a way for a retry that changed the output to say so. Fixing G14 alone would mean deleting sightings for a tool whose result regressed, which is a retention decision that belongs with M9's, and deleting evidence of a security finding having been observed is the kind of change that should not ride inside another issue. Nothing is broken today for the same reason G13 is not. Re-read at **M9.1**, alongside G13.
Note: G13 and G14 are the same window seen from two tables, and they are registered separately because their `Blocks-if-unresolved:` differ — G13 corrupts what a finding *says*, G14 corrupts *when it was seen*. M9.1 needs both, and closing one does not close the other.

### G15 — A transient normalization failure that outlives arq's retries is never recovered
Confirmed: M4.4, M4.5, post-M4, M8.8 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **a scan's findings are never produced, and nothing in the system will ever produce them — recovery requires a human to notice a `normalization_runs` row and read its `failure_reason`. There is no automatic path back.** This issue's entire subject is not losing owed work silently, and it ships one path that loses it. Stated that way round deliberately: "the sweep does not select `failed` rows" describes the predicate and reads like a tuning choice, while what it *means* is that a transient failure which exhausts arq's retries is permanently unrecovered. Worse than it sounds today, because **nothing surfaces `NormalizationRun` at all** — `failure_reason` is reachable only by querying Postgres by hand until M4.5 ships an endpoint, so "a human reads it" currently means a human who already suspected something was wrong. The mechanism: ADR-0021 decision 4 splits failures on whether they are deterministic in the persisted `ScanResult` rows. A *transient* one (a DB error, a Redis blip during the work, anything unanticipated) writes `failed` and re-raises so arq retries; after `max_tries` — arq's default 5 — the row is left `failed` for good. `get_stale` deliberately excludes `failed`, because the *deterministic* failure decision 5 describes would otherwise be re-enqueued every five minutes forever. So the exclusion is right for one of the two things that write `failed` and wrong for the other, and **nothing on the row records which it was**. The scan's findings are never produced, `normalization_runs` says `failed`, and M5 correlates over a project whose latest scan contributed nothing — with nothing raising and nothing marking the result incomplete. This is the same failure shape as G4, arriving one stage later through a different door.
Deferral rationale: the fix is a design question, not a predicate change, and the three candidates are not equivalent. (1) **Record the cause** — a marker distinguishing "deterministic, do not retry" from "transient, retry later" — which is the honest fix and means a new column plus a domain field, i.e. the shape ADR-016 decision 3 refuses to add speculatively. (2) **Bound the sweep's re-enqueues per row** — a retry count on `normalization_runs`, which turns the deterministic loop into a bounded one and needs its own limit chosen. (3) **An operator-facing requeue**, which is really an M8 dashboard feature. Choosing between them needs a production deployment to say how often transient failures actually exhaust arq, and none exists — the same reason G4 is deferred. **Nothing is broken today**: no deployment exists to fail transiently. Note the usual consolation does not fully apply — a `failed` row is *durable*, but it is not *visible* to anyone who is not already looking, which is the gap M4.5 narrows and does not close. Re-read at **M4.5** (which surfaces `NormalizationRun` and so is the first place a human could act on `failure_reason`), at **M8** (a requeue control), and immediately if a production `normalization_runs.failure_reason` is ever observed.
Note: found by `architecture-guardian` review of M4.4, not by a test — the sweep's own docstring and ADR-0021 both asserted "the worst case of an under-eager sweep is a scan that is never normalized" while shipping a code path that produces exactly that. Both were corrected in the same change rather than left to agree with each other and disagree with the code. **`--no-fix` note for whoever takes this:** widening `get_stale` to include `failed` is the one-line change that looks correct and is not — it converts the deterministic case into an unbounded five-minute retry loop, which is why option (1) or (2) has to come first.
Note (M4.5): **re-read as this entry instructed, and narrowed rather than closed — with one sentence above now false and corrected here.** `Blocks-if-unresolved:` says `failure_reason` is "reachable only by querying Postgres by hand until M4.5 ships an endpoint". M4.5 shipped it: `GET /projects/{id}/findings` returns the project's latest `NormalizationRun` — `status`, both timestamps, `failure_reason` — plus `unfinished_runs`, a count of every run that has not reached `completed`. The count is the half that matters, and `latest_run` alone would not have done: a project whose most recent scan normalized cleanly while three earlier ones failed reports `completed` and looks healthy. **What is unchanged is everything else in this entry.** The sweep still excludes `failed`, so a transient failure that exhausts arq's retries is still never re-enqueued and still needs a human. The three candidate fixes are untouched and still need a production deployment to choose between. So M4.5 moved this from "invisible and unrecovered" to "visible and unrecovered", which is worth having and is not a resolution. Re-read at **M8** (a requeue control) as before.
Note (post-M4): **M5.2 inherits the question M4.5 answered, and answering it once does not answer it again.** The findings listing says whether it may be incomplete — `latest_run` plus `unfinished_runs` — precisely because a list shortened by this gap is indistinguishable from a clean project. A Risk listing has the identical exposure and one more layer of it: a Risk is only as complete as the findings it correlated, so an unrecovered normalization can make a Risk *look* fully evidenced while a constituent finding was never produced. Nothing in M5.2's original entry said so, which is why it now does. This does **not** make the entry critical path for M5 — the recovery half still needs a production deployment to choose between its three candidate fixes, exactly as before — it makes M5.2 the second consumer that has to decide what its response admits.

Note (2026-09-19, post-M7 boundary review) · Confirms: none: **deferred under Rules for M8 rule 1.** M8 ships product only, and this entry does not trace to M8's exit condition. Trigger: **the M8→M9 boundary review**.

Note (2026-09-19, M8.8) · Confirms: M8.8: **strengthened: "outlives arq's retries" is every transient failure, because arq 0.28 retries none of them.**
