- **The fact.** arq 0.28.0 retries a job only on `Retry`, `RetryJob` or `CancelledError`. `NormalizeScanUseCase`'s transient branch re-raises an ordinary exception, so the run is left `failed` on its first attempt. The `max_tries = 5` this entry's Blocks-if-unresolved describes never applies.
- **What is unchanged.** The mechanism (`get_stale` excludes `failed`), the three candidate fixes, and the `--no-fix` warning against widening `get_stale`. The deferral under Rules for M8 rule 1 stands, with its trigger at the M8→M9 boundary review.
- **Where it is recorded.** ADR-0017's and ADR-0021's 2026-09-19 amendments. The two src sites that assert the retry, `normalize_scan.py` and `sweep_pending_normalizations.py`, are corrected in M8.8's code commit.
- **Found** by `architecture-guardian` on M8.8's documentation commit, whose first draft said normalization was not affected.

### G16 — `payload_truncated` is inferred from a parse attempt, not recorded
Confirmed: M4.5 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **the evidence route reporting `false` for a payload that IS truncated, silently, so a client parses an incomplete security finding as a complete one.** `GET /projects/{id}/findings/{fid}/evidence` returns `raw_payload` as an opaque string plus `payload_truncated`, derived by attempting `json.loads` and treating failure as truncation. That is exact **only while a character slice is the sole lossy step**: all three mappers build the payload with `json.dumps` and then apply `[:MAX_RAW_PAYLOAD_CHARS]`, so a cut payload cannot parse. The moment a mapper truncates at an *element* boundary instead — dropping whole instances from a ZAP alert, say — the result parses while still being incomplete, and this derivation reports `false`. Nothing raises, and the field's own name would then be a false statement about a finding's evidence. The response schema tells clients to parse when the flag is false, so the wrong answer is acted on rather than merely stored.
Deferral rationale: the honest fix is a stored `Evidence.truncated` column set by the mapper that did the truncating, and it is refused now **on a measurement rather than a preference**: across the three committed fixtures the largest payload ever produced is **9,888 characters against a 20,000 cap — 49%**, so the slice has never fired and the column would describe a state nothing has ever reached. That is the speculative shape ADR-016 decision 3 refuses and ADR-0021 refused again for `skipped_count`. The other candidate, `len(raw_payload) == MAX_RAW_PAYLOAD_CHARS`, is worse than either: it *fabricates* truncation for a payload landing exactly on the cap, which ADR-0019's principle ranks above under-counting. Trigger: **any change to how a mapper truncates**, and specifically any move to element-boundary truncation. Also re-read if a real capture ever produces a payload near the cap, since that is when the latent case becomes live.
Note: the field is named for what it asserts — "this payload is an incomplete prefix of its source element" — rather than for the test that currently detects it, precisely so the name stays true when the derivation stops being. Naming it `payload_parses_as_json` would have made the divergence invisible, because that name would still be accurate while the useful claim silently was not. See ADR-0022 decision 1.

### G17 — Two routes answer the same authorization question differently, so neither answer holds
Confirmed: M4.5, post-M4, M5.2, M6.3, M7.2, M8.8 · Status: assigned → M10.2
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **the 404 conceals nothing while a sibling route still answers 403 — so the property it was chosen for is defeated by one extra request.** M4.5's findings routes answer **404** for a project that does not exist and for one the caller may not read, indistinguishably, because `ProjectAccessPort.may_read_project` returns a single bool and has no vocabulary for the difference. `projects`' own routes answer **403** for an existing project the caller is not a member of. A caller denied `GET /projects/{id}/findings` with 404 calls `GET /projects/{id}/security-context`, gets 403, and knows the project exists. The concealment is real only if every project-scoped route agrees, and today they do not. Secondary cost, smaller but present from day one: a client gets two different answers to one question depending which route it hit, which is its own leak by difference and makes error handling in M8.3's frontend inconsistent.
Deferral rationale: the fix is a convergence and **which way to converge is G18's decision, not this entry's** — that is why the two are separate. If M10.2 decides the enumeration leak is acceptable for `projects`' routes, this entry is closed by moving the findings routes to 403; if it decides otherwise, this closes as a side effect of G18. Either way it needs the RBAC audit's own judgement about what a non-member should see across the whole surface, which is exactly M10.2's scope ("verify enforcement at every endpoint") and not something to settle inside a findings issue. Nothing is *worse* than before in the meantime: M4.5's routes are the strictly more conservative of the two, and the pre-existing leak (G18) is unchanged by them.
Note (post-M4): **the divergence widens with every module that copies ADR-0022 decision 2, and this entry did not say so.** That decision is explicitly "the shape M5.2, M6.3, M7.2 and M8.2 copy", and each of those ships a project-scoped route answering 404 for both denials — so M5.2 makes it three routes against `projects`' six, and by M8.2 it is six against six. Two consequences worth having in writing before M10.2 reads this. First, the cost of leaving it open is not static: every milestone adds a route whose 404 is defeated by the same one extra request, so the "conceals nothing" property gets *more* expensive to have been wrong about, not less. Second, the fix does not get harder in proportion — the findings side converges by changing one port's contract and the routes that consume it, which is why this stays `assigned → M10.2` rather than being pulled forward. What would change that judgement is M10.2 slipping past M8.2.
Note (2026-08-26, M5.2): **the forecast in the note above came true.** `GET /projects/{id}/risks` is the third route answering 404 for both denials, against `projects`' six still answering 403 — so a caller still recovers a project's existence with one extra request, now from one more starting point. Status unchanged: `assigned → M10.2`, and the convergence direction is still G18's.
Note: distinct from **G18**, and folding them together would blur a divergence this commit introduced with a leak that predates it by three milestones. Same symptom seen from two ends; different causes, and their fixes point in opposite directions. Split on G6/G7's precedent, where one `Blocks-if-unresolved:` covering both would have described neither. See ADR-0022 decision 2.

Note (2026-09-16, M6.3): **the forecast came true a second time, and the forecast's own arithmetic is stale.** `GET /projects/{id}/scored-risks` is the **fourth** route answering 404 for both denials, inheriting ADR-0022 decision 2 indirectly — `risk_engine` consumes no `ProjectAccessPort`, so the refusal reaches it through `CandidateRiskPort` as `CandidateRiskAccessDenied` and the route maps that to 404 (ADR-0030 decision 1). A caller still recovers a project's existence with one extra request, now from one more starting point. **What needs correcting is this entry's own post-M4 note**, which says *"M5.2 makes it three routes against `projects`' six, and by M8.2 it is six against six"*: **G18**'s dated note of 2026-09-09 retired *six* in favour of **eight**, so today's count is four against eight and the forecast's tidy symmetry at M8.2 never arrives. That note is left as written, on the same ground **G34** gives — a dated note is a record of what was believed then — and the correction lives here instead. Status unchanged: `assigned → M10.2`, and the convergence direction is still G18's to choose.

Note (2026-09-17, M7.2): **the forecast came true twice more. Six routes now answer 404 for both denials, against `projects`' eight answering 403.**
- **The two new routes.** `POST /projects/{project_id}/briefs` inherits the shape indirectly, through `ExplainableRiskPort` → `CandidateRiskPort`, as `/scored-risks` does. `GET /projects/{project_id}/briefs` consumes `ProjectAccessPort` directly, because it reads only `brief`'s own table.
- **Pinned.** Both are covered by `test_a_non_member_and_an_absent_project_are_indistinguishable` in `tests/integration/test_security_brief_routes.py`. Mapping the GET denial to 403 was applied as a mutation and killed.
- **Unchanged.** Status stays `assigned → M10.2`.

Note (2026-09-19, M8.8) · Confirms: M8.8: **two more are decided, the seventh and eighth routes answering 404 for both denials, against `projects`' eight answering 403.**
- **The two routes, landing in M8.8's code commit.** `POST /projects/{project_id}/scans` and `GET /projects/{project_id}/scans/{scan_id}` (ADR-0035 decisions 1, 2 and 4). The POST will consume the new `may_manage_project` verdict, the GET `may_read_project`, both directly.
- **What is new about the POST.** It will be the first 404-for-both route whose denial covers a member: a member who is not an owner gets the same 404 as a stranger, although they can read the project through its other routes. That leaks nothing, since the member already knows the project exists. It does make this entry's convergence one route wider.
- **Unchanged.** Status stays `assigned → M10.2`.

### G18 — `projects`' project-scoped routes leak project existence to a non-member
Confirmed: M4.5 · Status: assigned → M10.2
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **project-id enumeration across six routes, and it has been there since M1.4/M2.** Every project-scoped route in `projects` maps `ProjectNotFound` → **404** and `InsufficientPermissions` → **403**, so any authenticated user can distinguish "this project id exists" from "it does not" for a project they have no access to, by reading the status code. `connect_repository`, `connect_repository_via_github`, `detect_security_context`, `get_security_context`, `update_exposure_tags` and `update_scanner_config` all do it. ***(Note 2026-09-09, M5.5: **six is now eight.** `declare_serving` and `get_serving_declaration` map the same two exceptions the same two ways, so this entry's count and its list are both updated rather than left as a figure a later commit falsified. Nothing else about the entry changes — the mechanism, the deferral and the M10.2 assignment are unaffected by two more instances of one shape, and the audit that fixes it now has eight routes to read rather than six.)*** Ids are UUIDs, so this is not a practical enumeration of the whole space — what it is, is an oracle: a caller who *obtains* an id (a shared link, a log, a former membership) can confirm the project still exists and keep confirming it after their access is removed. `PRODUCT_SPEC.md` §11.2 puts least privilege and project-level RBAC together, and confirming existence is a privilege. It also **blocks G17**: that divergence cannot be closed until somebody decides which of the two behaviours is the right one.
Deferral rationale: changing six routes' error mapping is a behaviour change across a module M4.5 does not otherwise touch, and it is the kind that belongs with the audit that reads every endpoint rather than riding inside a findings issue — the same reasoning G3 uses for the CI action majors and G12 for the ruff pin. It also is not obviously a change to make in one direction: 404-for-both costs real diagnostic clarity for legitimate users who mistype an id, and that trade is worth making deliberately once, for the whole surface, rather than route by route. **Not folded into G17** because the causes differ (this predates M4.5 entirely) and because closing this as "accepted" would still leave G17 open. Assigned to **M10.2**, whose stated scope is verifying enforcement at every endpoint.
Note: found while deciding M4.5's own 404/403 answer, which is the useful part — the new route's design forced a comparison nobody had made, and the comparison found something older and larger than the thing being designed. Nothing here is a regression: this behaviour is unchanged since it was written.

### G19 — Synthetic data whose SHAPE differs from production makes a benchmark read as evidence while measuring the generator
Confirmed: M4.5, post-M4, M5→M6 boundary, M6.3, demo seed script, M5.3, M6.2 · Status: **open — was assigned to M5.3, which shipped without discharging it; next occurrence M6.2**
Kind: fact · Reopens if: anybody proposes a mechanical check that generated data reproduces the shape of production data — cardinality, ordering, distribution, width
Assignment (post-M4 boundary review): **this entry named M5.3 as its next occurrence, and M5.3 is now in the milestone starting** — so it moves from "re-read there" to acceptance criteria there, which is the difference between a warning and a thing somebody has to satisfy. One property sharpens it beyond what the rationale below anticipated: **M5.3 depends on M5.1, not M5.2.** The regression suite therefore runs against pure domain logic with constructed fixtures, before any persistence exists — so the fixtures are not one input among several, they are the **only** input, and there is no second source that could contradict a wrong shape. At M4.5 the blast radius was a slow query blessed by a benchmark, with real `EXPLAIN` plans available to catch it. Here it is a matcher blessed by its own fixtures, in an artifact this roadmap explicitly intends to show to people.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **any future performance or correctness decision taken against generated data — silently, and with a number that looks like proof.** It has already happened once, in the commit that opened this entry, and the near-miss is the entry: `scripts/seed_findings_benchmark.py` first generated finding ids **sequentially**, which clustered one project's findings at the head of any id-ordered scan and let the sighting join terminate almost immediately. The listing measured **8.8 ms** — at full volume (100k findings / 300k sightings), against real Postgres, under `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE, BUFFERS)`. Everything about that reading looked like evidence. Production ids are UUIDs (rule 9), which do not cluster; with UUID-shaped ids the identical query measured **763 ms**, a seq scan of 300,000 rows with a 22 MB external merge sort. **The shipped query would have been the 763 ms one, blessed by a benchmark, with an ADR quoting 8.8 ms.** The general form: the planner reads *shape* — cardinality, ordering, distribution, width — so a synthetic value differing from production in a way that looks cosmetic changes the plan without changing anything a reviewer would look at. Volume is what everyone checks and is not sufficient.
Deferral rationale: **there is no mechanical check for this and it is not clear one can exist** — it is a statement about whether generated data resembles data that does not exist yet, which is why the mitigation is a documented habit rather than a gate. What is *done* rather than deferred: the lesson is written at the top of the one generator this project has, at the two call sites that carry it, in ADR-0022's Consequences and in ADR-0020's Amendments. What is deferred is any general mechanism. Re-read **whenever anybody writes or extends a data generator, fixture factory, or seed script** — and specifically at **M5.3**, which the roadmap defines as "a curated fixture set (deliberately vulnerable sample app findings) with expected correlation groupings, used as a regression suite", explicitly a demonstrable proof artifact. A correlation regression suite whose synthetic findings lack a property real findings have would prove correlation works on data the system will never see, and that is the same failure with a worse blast radius than a slow query: it would be cited in interviews.
Note: **this is the third instance of one pattern, which is the reason it is named rather than left as a comment.** G8 is a verification invalidated by a tool (`ruff --fix` rewriting a test into a weaker one that still passes). G9 is a verification invalidated by a redacted fixture (the committed Semgrep `path` exercising the shape the fix would produce while production emitted the shape it would not, so every mapper test was green about a case that did not exist). G19 is a verification invalidated by a generator. All three are "sound verification, disconnected from reality", and each was found by accident rather than by a check. Their triggers differ and that is why they stay separate: G8's is `--fix`, G9's was a fixture re-capture, and this one's is *the next time somebody writes a seed script* — a moment no existing entry would have caused anyone to re-read. A comment in `seed_findings_benchmark.py` cannot reach M5.3, because M5.3 will write a different file.
Note (M5.3): **what M5.3 shipped, and which instruction it answers — recorded here because the answer is that this entry is not discharged.** `tests/unit/test_correlation_accuracy.py` checks each hand-written fixture's field **presence** per tool — populated or absent, over the profile's fields — against a profile derived at run time from the three committed captures in `tests/fixtures/scanners/`, per `docs/adr/0026-fixture-shape-conformance-check.md`. That satisfies the instruction in M5.3's bullet beginning *"Concretely: real ids are UUIDs (rule 9)"* — *"State which properties of real findings the fixtures reproduce and which they do not"* — which is now mechanical rather than a documented habit. **It does not satisfy this entry's assignment**, which asks about cardinality, ordering, distribution and width; ADR-0026's `### 3. What this check cannot assert` names all four as outside it, and gives the grounds. **Id shape in particular is unreachable from any corpus** — ids come from `IdGeneratorPort`, so they are not a property of captured scanner output at all — and **rule 9 remains its only source**.
Note (2026-09-15, M5→M6 boundary): **re-confirmed, and its assignment withdrawn rather than left pointing at a shipped issue.** M5.3 shipped, and its own `Note (M5.3)` says it does not satisfy this entry, so `assigned → M5.3` named a closed issue that had not discharged it — which `check_assignments_name_real_issues` cannot see, since M5.3 is a real heading. The next occurrence is M6.2's hand-built fixtures, carried in that issue's text. At three confirmations this entry passes the escalation guard on its `Deferral rationale:`.

Note (2026-09-16, M6.1): **the M6.2 occurrence this entry names now has a named artifact rather than a warning.** ADR-0005 decision 5 records that `UNKNOWN` and `CRITICAL` are **0 of 34 and 0 of 37** across both corpora, so two branches of the scoring function — the `UNKNOWN` rule and the top of the scale — are unreachable from any captured data and must be exercised by **synthetic** findings. That is this entry's exact subject arriving where it predicted, and it is now acceptance criteria in M6.2's own text rather than a re-read. Status is unchanged: no assignment, because M6.2 has not run.

Note (2026-09-16, M6.2): **the occurrence this entry predicted arrived in a DIFFERENT type than predicted, and the prediction's own terms were met.** M6.2 adds **no hand-written `Finding` at all** — every finding in `tests/unit/test_compute_risk.py` comes through the real mapper from a committed capture, and every group through correlation's real `build_match_key`/`group_by_match_key`, so shape is production's by construction rather than by conformance check. **G40** is therefore untouched: M6.2 adds nothing to the class it counts. Its figure is deliberately not restated here, because that entry's own `Note (M5.3)` records the ratio in its title as no longer current and not re-derived. What M6.2 does add is hand-written **`SurfaceMember`** values in `tests/unit/test_risk_scoring.py`, and two of those combinations are ones no pipeline can emit: `SurfaceMember(source=ZAP, severity=CRITICAL)` (ADR-0018 decision 1 gives ZAP a `HIGH` ceiling) and a Trivy member sharing a surface with a Semgrep one (their match keys can never be equal). They are exercised deliberately, because the arithmetic admits them and an untested branch of a scoring function is worse than a labelled synthetic input — so the `parametrize` ids carry the word `UNREACHABLE` and name why. That is this entry's defect one type down: the carrier changed, the failure mode did not. The pipeline-level consequence is **G64**. The `next occurrence M6.2` pointer in the `Status:` line above is now spent; no assignment was ever live to discharge, per the 2026-09-15 note.

Note (2026-09-16, M6.3): **the trigger fired on its own literal terms — somebody extended the generator — and the answer is short precisely BECAUSE no generated finding value moved.** `scripts/seed_findings_benchmark.py` gains two rows per project, a `projects` row and an owner `project_memberships` row, because `ProjectAccessPort` refuses a project-scoped read without one and M6.3's deliverable is the measurement of a **request**. Every finding, evidence and sighting value is what it was, so the shape question this entry exists to force — which properties of real findings the generated ones reproduce and which they do not — is **unchanged rather than re-answered**, and the UUID-shaped id line that opened this entry is untouched and still the load-bearing one. **What the extension does add is a second instrument, `_measure_reads`, and the reason it was added belongs in THIS entry rather than in G61's.** The first reading taken from the extended script compared `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)`'s **server-side** `Execution Time` against **end-to-end** request wall time and reported the difference as though the two were commensurable — omitting driver round-trip, row transfer and ORM hydration, which at 2,000 rows are not a rounding error. That is this entry's exact failure shape, sound-looking verification disconnected from what it covers, arriving this time through the **report** rather than through the generated data; it was caught in review before it reached a register note, and `_measure_reads` now times the read the same way the routes are timed so the two are comparable at all. **M6.3 is appended to `Confirmed:` above** — this entry was re-encountered and deferred again, which is what the register's own rule asks for, and the guardian round caught that three sibling entries got that append while the one describing the failure that actually happened did not. Two things about the `Status:` line are left as they stand: its `next occurrence M6.2` pointer is spent, as this entry's own M6.2 note already records, and its withdrawn M5.3 assignment is history — rewriting either would edit a record rather than add to it, which is **G34**'s distinction. Status otherwise unchanged: no mechanical check for this exists and none is proposed.

Note (2026-09-16, demo seed script): **the trigger fired — somebody wrote a seed script — and the answer is short because this one invents no finding value.** `scripts/seed_demo_project.py` REPLAYS the committed real capture of `verion-demo-target` (one Scan, three tools, `tests/fixtures/scanners/`), so the findings' shape is production's by construction rather than by a generator's assumptions.
- **Real:** the project and detect routes, `GitHubAdapter`'s tarball parsing and route extraction, `RunScanUseCase`, `NormalizeScanUseCase`, and correlation and scoring at read time.
- **Replaced:** GitHub, served from the committed tarball; the git checkout; and the scanner processes, whose committed output is replayed.
- **What it inherits instead is the capture's own limits:** one small Flask target, under the passive scan plan. A re-capture moves them, and **G39** and **G44** already track that, so this entry gains nothing to watch.

### G20 — Two ADRs defer on a fixture measurement that nothing asserts, so a re-capture changes it silently
Confirmed: post-M4, M5.1, M5.3 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **a latent failure becomes live with nothing announcing it, in the one code path this project deliberately left unguarded.** Two decisions rest on a single number — *all 30 elements across the six committed fixtures carry their tool's identifier (`check_id` / `VulnerabilityID` / `alertRef`), 0 missing*. **(M5.1: now 39 elements, still 0 missing — re-derived after G23's re-capture; see the Note below. The number moved, the property held, and nothing mechanical would have told anyone either way, which is this entry's whole point.)** ADR-0019 decision 2 uses it to bound `rule_id`'s `"(unidentified)"` fallback as "unreached by anything ever captured", saying explicitly that "if a re-capture ever brings an element without one, that is the signal to stop treating this as latent". ADR-0021 then rejects a `skipped_count` column on the same basis, "revisit if a real capture ever reaches the fallback". **No test asserts that measurement, and what stands in for it covers only some elements by accident** — 13 of 30 when this entry was written, 21 of 39 after M5.1 (see the Note below). *(An earlier draft of this entry said "neither signal exists … with a green suite throughout". That is wrong, and it is wrong in the direction this entry is about, so it is corrected rather than softened — an entry whose subject is documents asserting fixture properties nothing checks must not itself assert a coverage claim nothing checks.)* What actually exists:
- **Trivy's real vulnerabilities are guarded incidentally** — 12 when this was written, **20** since M5.1. `tests/unit/test_trivy_mapper.py` compares `{f.rule_id for f in findings}` against the `VulnerabilityID`s read out of the fixture at run time — so a re-capture dropping one raises `KeyError`, and an empty one makes `rule_id == "(unidentified)"` and the sets diverge. Red either way. But that test is named and written for *identity cardinality*, not for this precondition, so nothing connects its failure to the two ADRs that would have been invalidated.
- **Semgrep's 1 real element** is covered the same accidental way, by `assert finding.rule_id == "dangerous-eval"`.
- **ZAP's alerts are not covered** (4 when this was written, **5** since M5.1) — only `10021` is asserted, and the mapper has a second fallback to `pluginid`, so the identifier can go absent without any assertion noticing.
- **None of the 13 synthetic-edge elements are covered**, and those are the ones a future issue is most likely to extend.
The only `"(unidentified)"` assertion in the suite is `test_normalize_scan.py`'s, over a *synthetic* pair constructed to exercise the skip branch, which passes identically whether the committed fixtures carry identifiers or not.
So a re-capture that brings an identifier-less element makes two ADRs' stated preconditions false, arms a data-reachable path into `collapse_by_identity`'s raise, and turns `skipped_count`'s "describes a state nothing has ever reached" into a false statement. For ZAP and for every synthetic element that happens with a **green suite**; for Trivy and Semgrep it goes red under a test whose message will point at identity cardinality rather than at the thing that broke. The consequence downstream is a `NormalizationRun` marked `failed` with findings silently dropped from a scan, which is the invisible loss this register exists to prevent. **A guard written for another purpose is not a guard for this one** — that is the whole argument for the three-line test below, and it is a better argument than "nothing covers it" would have been.
Deferral rationale: **the fix is one parametrised test and the entry is registered rather than fixed only because a boundary review does not fix things outside its own documents.** It belongs beside the mapper tests, asserting over all six committed fixtures that every mapped `Finding.rule_id != "(unidentified)"` — the same construction ADR-0018 decision 4 already argues for at length in the sibling case: a literal assertion against a fixture read at run time, so a re-capture fails it. Whoever adds it should mutation-verify it the way that one was. **The contrast is the whole point of this entry**: the CWE-cardinality measurement in the same milestone got exactly this guard and an explicit argument for why it needed one (`test_the_real_cwe_cardinality_is_one_apart_from_a_single_two`, so named since M5.1), while the identifier measurement — load-bearing for two ADRs rather than one — got none. And the event class is not hypothetical: **M4.4 re-captured the Semgrep fixture**, correcting `check_id` and `path` to their production shapes, which is precisely the operation that would have needed this guard to fire. Re-read whenever any fixture in `tests/fixtures/scanners/` is re-captured or added, and at **M5.3**, which writes a new fixture set — see **G19**, whose assignment there makes that the natural moment to add both guards at once.
Note: distinct from **G19**, though they are neighbours and were found in the same pass. G19 is about generated data whose *shape* differs from production; this is about *committed real* data whose properties two documents assert without checking. G19's mitigation is a documented habit because no mechanical check is possible; this one's is a three-line test, which is what makes leaving it unwritten harder to defend rather than easier. They share a trigger only at M5.3.
Note (M5.1) — **the trigger fired, on the sibling measurement, and it fired LOUDLY because that one had the guard this entry exists to ask for.** G23's capture replaced all three real fixtures, and `CVE-2024-49767` (Werkzeug 2.3.8) carries `["CWE-400", "CWE-770"]` — so `test_every_real_vulnerability_carries_exactly_one_cwe`'s `assert counts == {1}` becomes `{1, 2}` and goes red. That test's own docstring predicted this in as many words: *"a re-capture that brought a two-CWE vulnerability fails here rather than silently widening the evidence the ADR rests on. Verified by mutation."* **This is the entry's argument confirmed rather than a new gap**, and it is recorded here rather than opened as one: the guarded measurement announced its own invalidation, which is precisely what the unguarded identifier measurement above still cannot do. The commit where that failure is visible is **`00708b9`** — deliberately a commit whose suite is red, so the guard's firing is an event in history rather than only a diff hunk where `{1}` became `{1, 2}`, which is indistinguishable from someone loosening an assertion that was in the way.
- **What it did *not* invalidate**, stated so the correction is not read as wider than it is: ADR-0018 decision 4's *choice* — `Finding.cwe` as a single `str | None` — stands. The mapper takes the first `CweIDs` entry and the full list survives in `Evidence.raw_payload`, and `test_multiple_cwes_keep_the_first_and_lose_none` already pinned that behaviour against synthetic input. What was falsified is the *fixture measurement the ADR cites as its basis*, which is exactly the class of claim this entry tracks.
- **One caveat the corpus cannot settle, recorded because it would otherwise be assumed.** The surviving CWE is the **first in document order** — that is what the mapper's `next(iter(...))` selects — but in this single case the first is also the numerically lowest (`CWE-400` before `CWE-770`), so **the data cannot distinguish "first" from "lowest"; only the code does.** Anybody re-deriving this from the fixture alone would be unable to tell which rule is in force. Whether that ordering is even stable across vulnerability-DB updates is a separate and unmeasured question — **G26**.
- The identifier measurement this entry is actually about was **re-checked against the new corpus and still holds, but its numbers all moved**: **26** real identifier-bearing elements (Semgrep 1 `check_id`, Trivy 20 `VulnerabilityID`, ZAP 5 `alertRef`), **0 missing**, plus the 13 unchanged synthetic ones — so the figure this entry opened with, *"all 30 elements across the six committed fixtures"*, is now **39**. Re-derived rather than scaled: an earlier draft of this bullet wrote 34, which was the *finding* count (ZAP maps 5 alerts to 13 per-instance findings) and not the element count the identifier sits on. Still unguarded as to the identifier measurement itself — **and the accidental coverage moved twice in opposite directions, which is worth following.** At commit `00708b9` it got worse: `test_each_vulnerability_gets_its_own_identity` asserted `len({dedup_hash}) == 12` and built its expected set from `Vulnerabilities[i] for i in range(12)`: both hardcoded literals indexing the *first twelve* elements, not "however many the fixture holds", so the guard covered 12 of 20 by index and was red. **Commit `6d2503c` settled it in the direction this entry asks for**: the expected set is now derived from every `Vulnerabilities` entry in the document, so it tracks the fixture instead of a number somebody has to remember, and Trivy's incidental coverage is 20 of 20 rather than 12 of 20. *(An earlier draft of this bullet said "now covers 20 of them rather than 12" — scaled from the new fixture rather than read from the test, in the same bullet that holds itself to "re-derived rather than scaled". Corrected, and left visible: this entry is about figures asserted without re-derivation, so one of its own is worth showing rather than tidying away.)* The three-line test remains unwritten and remains this entry's ask.
Note (M5.1, second commit): **the stale `30` survived in three more places, which is this entry's thesis holding rather than a new gap — and each was found by a separate guardian pass, none by any gate.** The M5.1 re-capture updated ADR-0019's Amendments and two `ROADMAP.md` bullets and missed the rest, because *"update every copy"* is a manual sweep and **nothing enumerates the copies** — which is the whole reason this entry asks for a test rather than for more care. The full list, so the next sweep starts from an enumeration:
- `docs/adr/0021-normalization-job-execution.md`'s `skipped_count` rejection — **annotated in this commit.** Worth naming: this is the *second* of the two ADRs whose stated precondition this entry's `Blocks-if-unresolved:` explicitly names, so it was one of the two documents the entry has always been about, and it was still missed.
- `docs/adr/0019-finding-identity-and-deduplication.md`'s decision 2 — **annotated in this commit.** It was corrected in that document's own Amendments and nowhere inline. **A correction whose adequacy depends on which document it is in is not a standard**, and that inconsistency is the finding rather than the missing marker.
- **`src/verion/modules/normalization/domain/finding.py`'s `Finding` docstring still says `30`. Not fixed here** — this commit is documentation-only by its own scope and `src/` is outside it. It is the last copy **known** to be un-updated — the enumeration above is a record of what has been checked, not a claim of completeness. Correct it in the next commit that legitimately touches that file — ideally the one that finally writes this entry's three-line test, since both concern the same measurement.
Note (M5.3): **the trigger fired and the answer is that it is still not this issue's work, which is recorded rather than left as a silent skip.** This entry's re-read trigger names M5.3 as the issue that writes a new fixture set. M5.3 wrote one — `_CURATED` in `tests/unit/test_correlation_accuracy.py` — but wrote **no fixture under `tests/fixtures/scanners/`**, and this entry's subject is the identifier measurement over *committed real* captures, which M5.3 neither re-captured nor changed. So the three-line test remains unwritten and remains this entry's ask, unchanged by the artifact M5.3 shipped.

Note (2026-09-19, post-M7 boundary review) · Confirms: none: **deferred under Rules for M8 rule 1.** M8 ships product only, and this entry does not trace to M8's exit condition. Trigger: **the M8→M9 boundary review**.

### G21 — `pre-commit-hooks` is pinned two majors behind, and it is the one hook repo that rewrites files
Confirmed: M5.0, M5.9 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **nothing loudly, and the entry says so rather than inventing a consequence** — which is the honest form of `Blocks-if-unresolved:` for a gap whose cost is real but bounded. `.pre-commit-config.yaml` pins `pre-commit/pre-commit-hooks` at **v4.6.0**; upstream is at **v6.0.0** (confirmed by `git ls-remote --tags` at M5.0). Unlike ruff, these five hooks — `trailing-whitespace`, `end-of-file-fixer`, `check-yaml`, `check-added-large-files`, `check-merge-conflict` — have **no CI counterpart**, so there is no hook-versus-CI divergence class here and none of G12's failure mode applies: nothing can report green locally and red in CI on their account, because CI does not run them. What is real is that two of them **rewrite files on every commit**, so bumping two majors is a rewrite surface, and `check-yaml`'s parser behaviour across majors is exactly the sort of thing that changes quietly for a repo with four YAML files including `ci.yml`. There is already one visible symptom, surfaced at M5.0 the moment the hook cache was rebuilt: pre-commit itself now warns that **the pinned `pre-commit-hooks` repo** *"uses deprecated stage names (commit, push) which will be removed in a future version"* — the legacy `stages: [commit, push, manual]` is in v4.6.0's own manifest, on `trailing-whitespace`, `end-of-file-fixer` and `check-added-large-files`, not in Verion's config, so grepping this repo for `stages:` finds nothing and the fix is the bump. That is a **second entry in the register whose trigger fires without anybody acting** — the property that made G3 worth an issue — but a far weaker one: the failure is a loud tooling error on developer machines, not all of CI at once, and it names its own fix.
Deferral rationale: **it is the bundling M5.0 exists to avoid.** M5.0's whole argument is that G3 and G12 deserved a pass of their own rather than riding inside a persistence issue; adding an unrelated two-major bump of file-rewriting hooks to the issue that closes them repeats the mistake one level down, and `CLAUDE.md`'s "don't bundle unrelated changes" says so directly. The version skew that *mattered* — the one where a green hook did not mean a green build — is closed. Trigger: **the next time `.pre-commit-config.yaml` is opened for any reason**, or the first time one of these hooks rewrites something a person did not expect. Do it the way M5.0 did ruff: bump, run over the whole tree, read the diff before staging.
Note (2026-09-08, M5.9 commit 1): **the trigger fired on BOTH halves at once, and the bump is still not taken.** This commit opens the file — the first half — and it opens it *because* of the second: `trailing-whitespace` and `end-of-file-fixer`, two of the five hooks this entry names, would rewrite committed scanner evidence, since `tests/integration/fixtures/active_scan/` holds a ZAP report and an access log carrying attack payloads verbatim. Both are now `exclude`d from that path, with the reason in a comment beside them. **The second half fired in the preventive direction rather than the destructive one**, and the distinction is worth keeping: measured before the exclude was added, neither hook changes either file, so nothing was silently rewritten — what happened is that somebody looked at the hooks and found the input that *would* break. **Not discharged**: the version skew is untouched, `rev` is still `v4.6.0` against upstream's v6.0.0, the deprecated-stage-names warning still fires, and a two-major bump of file-rewriting hooks inside a capture commit is precisely the bundling this entry's own `Deferral rationale:` refuses. A comment on the `repo:` block now says so at the site, so the next person to open this file does not have to reach the register to learn the bump is deliberate.

### G22 — `actions/checkout@v7`'s fork-PR block is inert only while every workflow triggers on `pull_request`
Confirmed: M5.0 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **a future workflow silently not checking out the code it was written to check out.** checkout v7.0.0 blocks fork-PR checkout for `pull_request_target` and `workflow_run`, gated behind a new `allow-unsafe-pr-checkout` input defaulting to `false`. `ci.yml` triggers on `push` and `pull_request` only, so at M5.0 this change does **nothing** — which is why v7 was safe to take, and why the inertness is a property of today's triggers rather than of the action. The moment any workflow adds `pull_request_target` or `workflow_run` — the natural shapes for a job needing repository secrets, which is what M10.2 or a deploy pipeline would reach for — that job stops checking out fork PRs, and the failure presents as an empty or unexpected working tree rather than as a permissions error naming the cause.
Deferral rationale: there is nothing to fix. This is a deferral with a **trigger condition** rather than an open defect, recorded here because `CLAUDE.md`'s working-style rule says a trigger belongs in the register where it has a `Blocks-if-unresolved:` field and an escalation check, not in a comment or a commit message — the lesson G1, G2 and G4 each already record. When it fires the decision is deliberate and two-sided: either that job genuinely should not check out fork PRs (usually correct, and the reason upstream made this the default), or it must set `allow-unsafe-pr-checkout: true` knowing what that re-enables. Trigger: **any workflow gaining a `pull_request_target` or `workflow_run` trigger.**

### G23 — No captured scanner output exists for the three tools against a common target, so cross-tool correlation cannot be validated against real data
Confirmed: M5.1 · Status: resolved → M5.1
Resolution (M5.1): **captured.** `github.com/CarlosPD8/verion-demo-target` at `c68caa7aba8db8ae64da6dd2b4e1b8a05ecd1850`, scanned by one `RunScanUseCase.execute()` over one `GitRepoCheckout` with all three real adapters — Semgrep against `app.py`, Trivy against `requirements.txt` in that same tree, ZAP against that same tree served over HTTP by its own Flask app on the target's full pinned closure. `Scan` reached `completed`; 34 findings (Semgrep 1, Trivy 20, ZAP 13). The three committed fixtures and `tests/fixtures/scanners/README.md` are replaced wholesale. **One `Scan` rather than three runs was the requirement, not a convenience** — ADR-016 decision 1's snapshot coherence is what makes a cross-tool comparison over this corpus well-founded, and three separate runs would have been almost-but-not-quite what this entry asked for. ***(Narrowed 2026-08-25 by **G27**, and the narrowing matters because this sentence is the one a reader takes the corpus's admissibility from. That guarantee covers `REPO_PATH` scanners: ZAP is dispatched by `target_kind` to a URL and never reads the checkout, so the premise is established by construction for Semgrep↔Trivy and **assumed** for anything involving ZAP. It happens to hold for this capture — the app was served out-of-process from the checkout itself — which is precisely why nothing here noticed it was an assumption. Read "well-founded" as covering two tools of three.)***
- **Establishes:** cross-tool measurement over this corpus now measures *signals* rather than three unrelated captures. Concretely, the two claims M5.1 had to leave open are now answerable: the CWE intersection is **empty for all three pairs** (Semgrep contributes none at all — G6 — while Trivy's 14 and ZAP's 4 are disjoint), and Semgrep's `file_path` `app.py` against Trivy's `requirements.txt` is a source path against a manifest path, on one repository. Both were previously inadmissible for resting on unrelated targets; they are admissible now. ADR-0023's deferral of *which fields the match key carries* has the data it was waiting for.
- **Does NOT establish a SAST↔DAST pair over the same vulnerability, and no corpus ever will** — see **G24**, opened by this capture. ZAP crawled `/calculate?expr=2*3`, received a real response and reported nothing about the `eval()` sink, because the scan plan is passive-only. This entry must not be read as "the corpus is complete": it is complete as to *targets* and permanently incomplete as to *DAST depth*, and the second half is a scanner configuration rather than a capture problem.
- **What the admissible zero still does not carry.** 14 CWEs against 4, over one small Flask app with three vulnerable packages, is thin. The disjointness is *suggestive* of a structural split — Trivy's CWEs describe dependency weaknesses from CVE metadata, ZAP's describe header and configuration weaknesses from passive checks — but one application cannot separate "these families never overlap" from "this one happened not to". Deciding whether CWE is a viable cross-tool signal is **G6's** call at M5.1, which now at least has real data to make it on; this entry supplies the data and does not make the decision.
- **A limitation found while capturing, recorded here and registered as G25:** the pinned SHA could not be *requested*. `GitRepoCheckout` runs `git clone --depth 1` with no `--branch` and no `--revision`, so provenance was established by reading `git rev-parse HEAD` back out of the checkout and refusing to proceed unless it matched. That works only while `c68caa7` is the default-branch tip, and it is a symptom of `Scan` recording no commit at all.
- **Distinct from G24, G25 and G27 in the G9/G10 manner**, stated in the resolution so that arriving at a resolved entry still tells a reader the capture session produced **three** live gaps. *(G27 added 2026-08-25. It was not opened by the capture itself but by the measurement over it, which is a distinction worth keeping: G24 and G25 were visible while capturing, and G27 only became visible once somebody asked what made the cross-tool comparison valid.)* This entry's fix was *capturing*; G24's is a change to the adapter's scan plan and G25's is a missing domain field, and neither is discharged by any corpus however good.
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M5.1, M5.3, and the one product claim this roadmap promises to demonstrate.** Three separate things, because this gap is not "a fixture is missing" but "the evidence every correlation decision would be validated against does not exist".
- **M5.1** cannot validate its choice of matching signals. Any measurement over the current fixtures cannot distinguish *"these tools do not correlate"* from *"these captures describe different systems"*, so a signal chosen against them is chosen against the corpus rather than against reality. ADR-0023 defers **which fields the match key carries** on exactly this basis.
- **M5.3** is worse, and the reason is its own `Depends on:`. It depends on **M5.1, not M5.2**, so the suite runs against pure domain logic with constructed fixtures before any persistence exists — the fixtures are the matcher's **only** input, with no second source able to contradict a wrong shape. A curated set built to make correlation succeed would demonstrate exactly that and nothing else.
- **`PRODUCT_SPEC.md` §10's demonstration goal**, verbatim: *"At least one clear, demonstrable case where correlation across 2+ tools produces a materially better prioritization than looking at raw tool output."* That is the CV/portfolio artifact this roadmap explicitly intends to show people, and there is currently no captured data on which it could be produced honestly.
Evidence, from `tests/fixtures/scanners/README.md`'s own provenance table: Semgrep was captured against `tests/integration/fixtures/semgrep_target/vulnerable.py`, Trivy against `tests/integration/fixtures/trivy_target/requirements.txt`, and ZAP against a local `ThreadingHTTPServer` serving one static HTML page — **three unrelated targets**, all captured 2026-08-23. That same README records *why*, in its **"Why not `test_multi_scanner_dispatch.py`"** section: the roadmap originally asked for all three to be captured from that one test, and it does not work, because that test scans `github.com/octocat/Hello-World`, where "Semgrep returns `results: []` and Trivy finds no manifest" — "two of the three fixtures would have been empty envelopes".
Note — **the fix is not "one target", and stating it that way would describe something impossible.** `test_multi_scanner_dispatch.py` is the only artifact that runs all three real scanners over a single `Scan`, and even it does not put them on one target: ZAP receives `zap_target_url` (a local server) while Semgrep and Trivy receive the cloned repo. Its own docstring says why — ZAP "is the only URL-kind scanner", and the `REPO_PATH`-vs-`URL` split is what that test exists to exercise. So what is needed is **one artifact reachable two ways**: a tree Semgrep and Trivy can scan that is *also* served over HTTP.
Deferral rationale: naming the fix is cheap and building it is not this issue's job — M5.1 is a design issue whose output is an ADR, and a capture corpus is test infrastructure. The shape is already known and every piece of it already exists somewhere in this repo: Python source carrying a pattern the pinned `rulesets/default.yml` fires on (`vulnerable.py` is one), a `requirements.txt` with known-vulnerable pins (**`urllib3 1.24.1` is already measured to produce 12 findings**), and a servable HTTP surface over that same tree (the `ThreadingHTTPServer` shape `test_multi_scanner_dispatch.py` and `test_zap_adapter.py` both already use). Resolve at **M5.3 at the latest**, since that issue cannot produce an honest proof artifact without it.
Note: **distinct from G19**, and the distinction is stated so folding them later is a deliberate act rather than a tidy-up. They were found in the same measurement session and they are the G9/G10 pattern — same session, different field, different `Blocks-if-unresolved:`. **G19 is about fixture *shape* differing from production** (synthetic ids that cluster where real UUIDs do not), and its fix is to declare which properties of real data the fixtures reproduce. **This is about a fixture that does not exist at all.** G19's mitigation can be applied to a corpus; this one has to be captured first. If they are ever merged, the merged entry must keep both `Blocks-if-unresolved:` fields, because neither implies the other.

### G24 — `ZapAdapter`'s scan plan is passive-only, so DAST reports no injection finding and no SAST↔DAST pair over one vulnerability is obtainable from any corpus *while the plan stays passive*
Confirmed: M5.1 · Status: resolved → M5.9
Resolution: M5.9 — `tests/integration/test_active_scan_finds_the_sink.py`, which CI runs on every push to `main` and every pull request against it. **The DAST half now exists**: an alert whose `alertRef` is absent from the five in the committed passive `zap_scan.json`, on `/calculate`, attributed to `expr` through the instance's `param` field, produced by the shipped `ZapAdapter` on the plan `_build_plan_yaml` builds for a consented project. The SAST half was already committed against the same pinned SHA — `semgrep_scan.json`'s `dangerous-eval`. **What this does not close, and this entry's own `Note (M5.1)` predicted it in as many words:** nothing correlates the two. **G27 is unresolved and `assigned → M5.5`** — not `open`, which this register uses as a distinct value — so the pair exists and correlating it would still be unfounded, and it already has an owner for exactly that validity condition. *"Resolving G24 alone yields a real pair whose correlation is still unfounded"* is now the live situation rather than a forecast, and ADR-0023 Decision C keeps one of its two triggers. **That decision's own trigger table is left stale by this resolution and is not corrected here:** it reads *"Measured satisfiable by the probe above and still open — no `activeScan` job has shipped"*, which M5.4 already falsified and this entry falsifies a second time. Not fixed because M5.9 does not amend an ADR — **opened as G46**, which is where it survives, since a `Resolution:` paragraph inside a resolved entry carries no trigger and no reader ever arrives at it. Note also **what does the resolving**: the test asserts the *property* and none of the probe's figures — no alert id, riskcode, confidence, cweid, name or count, per ADR-0027 decision 5 — so this entry is closed by something reproducible rather than by the 2026-08-25 numbers it is otherwise full of.
Note (2026-08-27): the assignment moved from M5.4 to M5.9 when M5.4's scope was split.
**Title corrected 2026-08-25 and the correction is the entry's own subject.** It read *"…so DAST can **never** report an injection finding and no SAST↔DAST pair … is obtainable from **any corpus**"*. The second half is still true and is why this entry exists; the word "never" was not a claim about corpora at all but about the scan plan, and it was written as though the two were the same. Measured below: change the plan and DAST reports the injection. Rewritten rather than annotated, on the treatment this register gives a claim that turns out **wrong** rather than incomplete (G3's version targets, G4's proxy split).
Blocks-if-unresolved: **the single strongest demonstration this product promises, and it is unobtainable rather than merely missing.** Three named consequences:
- **M5.1's own worked example has no instance.** This milestone's entry offers *"same endpoint path referenced by SAST taint sink and DAST result"* as a correlation goal. Passive scanning observes responses and never exercises a sink, so DAST cannot produce the second half of that pair for any injection, on any target.
- **M5.3's proof artifact is bounded by it.** That issue exists to produce a demonstrable regression suite, and the best it can demonstrate is correlation by *derived location* — normalising a URL path against a file path, itself a derivation with its own false-positive behaviour — never correlation by shared vulnerability. A curated fixture set implying otherwise would be demonstrating something the system cannot do.
- **`PRODUCT_SPEC.md` §10, verbatim:** *"At least one clear, demonstrable case where correlation across 2+ tools produces a materially better prioritization than looking at raw tool output."* The most compelling such case — SAST finds `eval()`, DAST proves it reachable and exploitable — is exactly the one ruled out.
Evidence: `_build_plan_yaml`, a module-level function in `scanning/adapters/outbound/scanners/zap_adapter.py`, builds three jobs — `spider`, `passiveScan-wait`, `report` — and its own comment states the omission is deliberate: *"A bounded baseline-equivalent scan — spider + passive scanning only, deliberately no `activeScan` job (an active scan is unbounded in scope and makes real attack requests against the target; out of scope for this issue)."* **Measured, not inferred, by G23's capture:** ZAP crawled `/calculate?expr=2*3` — it appears in the committed fixture's alert instances alongside `/`, `/robots.txt` and `/sitemap.xml` — received a real response from the live Flask app, and reported nothing about the `eval()` behind it. Its five alerts are all response-level (CSP, anti-clickjacking, `Server` version disclosure, `X-Content-Type-Options`, suspicious comments). The tool saw the vulnerable endpoint and could not see the vulnerability.
Deferral rationale: **enabling `activeScan` changes what production scans do, and that is not a capture issue's decision to take.** An active scan makes real attack requests against a user-supplied target, is unbounded in scope, and carries a runtime cost against a step already dominated by container-bound tests — none of which ADR-013's SSRF gate addresses, since that gate governs *which* targets may be reached, not *what is done* to them. It would also need its own decision about scan policy and duration bounds. Trigger: **the first issue that needs a demonstrated SAST↔DAST pair** — M5.3 if its proof artifact claims one, M7.3's brief otherwise — or any proposal to add an active-scan job for another reason. Whoever takes it should read this entry together with `PRODUCT_SPEC.md` §11.1's threat-model-first principle, because pointing an active scanner at a user-supplied URL is a materially different act from crawling it.
Note: **distinct from G23, G25 and G27 in the G9/G10 manner** *(G27 added to this list 2026-08-25)* — G23, G24 and G25 surfaced in one capture session and G27 in the measurement over it and are separated deliberately so folding any pair later is an act rather than a tidy-up. G23 was a corpus that did not exist and was fixed by capturing one. G25 is a missing domain field, fixed by changing the entity and the checkout port. **This is a scanner configuration, fixed by changing the adapter's scan plan — and uniquely among the three, no corpus however well captured discharges it.** None implies the others, and a merged entry would have to keep every one of their `Blocks-if-unresolved:` fields.
Note (M5.1): **this entry is now one of two triggers on ADR-0023 Decision C, and the pairing is the point — resolving this one alone does not open the path.** That decision defers the SAST↔DAST derivation and names **this entry as its *existence* trigger** (passive scanning never exercises the sink, so no DAST finding about the `eval()` exists to correlate) and **G27 as its *validity* trigger** (nothing asserts the crawled URL serves the tree Semgrep read). Resolving G24 alone yields a real pair whose correlation is still unfounded; resolving G27 alone yields a valid path with nothing to put through it. Whoever comes here to enable an active scan should read G27 in the same sitting.
- **And a measurement that sharpens what this entry costs, taken on G23's corpus at M5.1.** It was already known that ZAP reported nothing about the sink. What is new is that the *fallback* — correlating by derived location, which this entry's own `Blocks-if-unresolved:` offers as the best M5.3 could demonstrate — is **empty of information on this corpus, before an active scan is even considered.** Four distinct URLs were crawled, and the per-URL alert sets are `/` → `{10020-1, 10021, 10027, 10036-2, 10038-1}`, `/calculate?expr=2*3` → `{10020-1, 10021, 10036-2, 10038-1}`, `/robots.txt` and `/sitemap.xml` → `{10036-2, 10038-1}` each. **No alert fires only on `/calculate`, and its alert set is a strict subset of `/`'s** — the site root carries every alert the vulnerable endpoint does, plus one more. So a derived group would be `{dangerous-eval}` plus four response-header alerts that all also fire on `/`: a co-location with no discriminating power, which does not meet `PRODUCT_SPEC.md` §10's "materially better prioritization" and would read as though it did. The fallback this entry names as a consolation is thinner than it sounds.

Note (2026-08-27, M5.4): **this entry's `Evidence:` describes a plan that is no longer what production builds, and the entry stays open anyway.** M5.4 shipped the `activeScan` job behind per-project consent, so on a consented project the plan is four jobs rather than the three that field enumerates, and the adapter comment it quotes verbatim — *"A bounded baseline-equivalent scan … deliberately no `activeScan` job …"* — was deleted by that commit, so the quotation no longer resolves against the tree. **What is unchanged is why this entry is open.** Its subject was never the plan's contents but the SAST↔DAST pair being unobtainable, and no such pair has been obtained: no capture exists under an active plan, and no committed test runs one. M5.4 made the pair obtainable; **M5.9** obtains it. `Evidence:` is annotated rather than rewritten, on the treatment this register gives a field that has become dated rather than wrong.

Note (2026-08-25, active-scan probe) — **the untested half of this entry is now MEASURED, and it is measured in the affirmative: ZAP's active scanner does detect the `eval()` sink.** A hand-run of `ZapAdapter`'s own plan shape with an `activeScan` job added, against the same `verion-demo-target` at `c68caa7`, on the same Flask app. What follows is the evidence the issue this entry is assigned to is built on.

- **The finding.** Alert **`90036` "Server Side Template Injection (Blind)"**, riskcode **3**, confidence **3**, param **`expr`**, on **`/calculate`**. Alert **`6-5` "Path Traversal"**, riskcode 3, confidence **1**, same param, same endpoint. Both are new; neither exists in the passive corpus.
- **Detection is by TIME, not by response matching**, which is why confidence is 3. Two `sleep 15` executions, each followed one second later by a `sleep 1` confirmation — visible in the target's access log as exactly two fifteen-second gaps (16:17:57→16:18:12 and 16:18:13→16:18:28) with the confirmations at 16:18:13 and 16:18:29. Replayed by hand afterwards with `sleep 3`. **Every other sleep-bearing payload returned instantly** — bash Shellshock, MySQL `sleep()`, HSQLDB `Thread.sleep`, Oracle `DBMS_SESSION.SLEEP`, `pg_sleep`, MSSQL `WAITFOR`, Node `execSync`, Ruby `%x()`, PowerShell `start-sleep`, Windows `timeout /T`, Freemarker, Velocity, XSLT and more. **That list is illustrative, not exhaustive, and they fail in two different ways.** Measured over the whole log: **71 distinct delay-bearing `expr` values** — literal `sleep`, MSSQL `WAITFOR DELAY`, Windows `timeout /T` — of which **10 parse** under `ast.parse(…, mode="eval")` and the other **61** are outright `SyntaxError`s, the `${…}` sibling of the working payload among them. *(Whole-log, so it includes the `sleep 3` hand replay described above; **ZAP itself sent 70**, of which 9 parse and 2 fired. Populations separated here for the same reason the blast-radius bullet separates them.)* *(A regex matching `waitfor` unanchored returns 72, the extra being an XSLT payload whose only delay-shaped token is `waitFor` in `process:waitFor($process)` and whose command is `erroneous_command`.)* **Three of the ten that parse are the `{{__import__…}}` payloads that fired**, so **68 payloads did not fire**. **Seven of those 68 parse and die at runtime rather than at parse time, spanning five families**: MySQL's `sleep(15)` and Oracle's `DBMS_SESSION.SLEEP(15)` raise `NameError`; HSQLDB's `"java.lang.Thread.sleep"(15000)` raises `TypeError: 'str' object is not callable`; and a `{system("sleep 15")}` and a Jinja2 `__class__.__mro__[…].__subclasses__()[157]` escape each raise their own runtime error (the second's exact type depends on the interpreter's subclass table, so it is deliberately not named). The HSQLDB pair leaves a fingerprint in the probe's own log: the two `SyntaxWarning: 'str' object is not callable; perhaps you missed a comma?` lines in `app.log` immediately precede those two requests — ten HSQLDB-shaped payloads were sent and exactly the two that parse produced a warning. **What matters is that none of the 68 non-firing payloads reaches a shell**, which holds whichever way each one fails.
- **The MECHANISM, and this paragraph is the reason the entry is written at this length.** ZAP classifies it **CWE-1336 (SSTI)**, yet **nothing in the target renders user input as a template.** Jinja2 3.1.6 *is* pinned and `app.py` calls `render_template` twice, so "no template engine" would be false — but both calls name fixed template files, neither passes user input through a template compiler, and there is no `render_template_string` or template sink of any kind. The sink reached from the query string is `eval`. The payload that fires is `{{__import__("subprocess").check_output("sleep 15", shell=True)}}`, and in **Python** `{{…}}` is a **set literal containing a set literal** — `ast.parse(…, mode="eval")` gives `Set(elts=[Set(elts=[Call(...)])])`. `eval` evaluates the inner expression, the subprocess runs, and only then does it die on `TypeError: unhashable type: 'set'`. **The payload proving RCE is shaped like a template payload by coincidence.** Without this sentence a later reader sees CWE-1336 on a Flask app and hunts a template sink that was never there.
- **ADR-0023 Decision C's measured basis is FALSIFIED. Which half died and which survived**, because the decision stands and a reader needs to know on what. **Died:** *"No alert fires only on `/calculate`, and its alert set is a strict subset of `/`'s."* Under active scan **two alerts fire only on `/calculate`** (`6-5`, `90036`) and the subset relation is **false in both directions** — `/` carries `10106` and `10027` the endpoint does not. The "zero discriminating power" conclusion drawn from it is therefore also false. **Survived:** the decision's *other* trigger, **G27**, is untouched — nothing about the probe says the crawled URL serves the tree Semgrep read. So Decision C's deferral is **unchanged in substance and changed in balance**, matching the ADR's own wording: **both triggers remain open, and "measured satisfiable" is not "satisfied"**. No `activeScan` job has shipped, so no production scan yields the DAST half yet. What changed is G24's *character* — from an open question about whether the finding is obtainable to a decision about scan policy with a measured answer behind it — not its status.
- **ADR-0023 Decision B's grounds (1) and (3) are also falsified**, which is larger than this entry's own subject and is recorded here because this entry is what falsifies them. Ground (3) — *"G24 bounds ZAP's reachable CWE vocabulary to passive response-configuration weaknesses permanently and independently of any corpus"* — is wrong: ZAP now emits **CWE-1336, CWE-22 and CWE-311** on top of the passive `{497, 615, 693, 1021}`. Ground (1)'s **surviving cell** — the Trivy↔ZAP `cwe` intersection, which Decision B's own re-read paragraph says is what stands even if G6 is resolved — is **no longer empty**: `CWE-22` is on both sides. **Decision B nevertheless survives with more force, on a ground it did not have**, and the ADR's amendment carries it: the pair is **ZAP `6-5` at confidence 1 with the literal attack payload `calculate`** — the URL's own last path segment, against an app that performs no file access on any user-controlled input at all — against **Trivy `CVE-2024-49766` (fixture `Title`: `werkzeug: python-werkzeug: Werkzeug safe_join not safe on Windows`)**. The only cross-tool CWE pair this project has ever measured is one side hallucinating.
- **Cost: 62s wall-clock, of which the `activeScan` job is 47s**, against 13–18s for the two existing passive ZAP tests. That is the input to **M5.7**'s projection, and it is a hand-run of a container rather than a CI step reading.
- **Blast radius, measured from the target's own access log rather than estimated: 391 well-formed requests plus 7 malformed TLS probes at the HTTP port, across 86 distinct paths.** Cloud-metadata SSRF probes for **six** cloud providers (AWS, GCP, Azure, Oracle, DigitalOcean, OpenStack), secret-file probes (`/.ssh/id_rsa`, `/.env`, `/.git/config`, `/key.pem`, `/server.key`, …), and real shell command execution on the target host. **Zero 5xx.** Across those 391 the status distribution is 200×252, 404×132, 405×7, plus the **400×7** of the malformed TLS probes counted separately above — so the 400s are not an additional failure class. *(252 is the count **inside the scan window**; the log's whole-file total is 257, the extra five being two pre-scan smoke requests and the three hand replays this note describes. Stated because 391 and a whole-file distribution do not sum, and the two populations are easy to mix.)* Nothing persisted; note that last clause is a property of *this* target, not a guarantee about any target.
- **A sixth alert appears that the committed corpus does not have**, recorded so a future re-capture reads it as explained rather than as drift: **`10106` "HTTP Only Site"** (CWE-311). **The direction is settled, and it is the opposite of the obvious reading — the alert caused the malformed probes, not the other way round.** `report.json`'s `10106` instance says so in its own record: `otherinfo` reads `"Failed to connect.\nZAP attempted to connect via: https://host.docker.internal:8080/"`. That is the rule doing what establishing "this site is HTTP only" requires — attempting HTTPS and failing — and it accounts for both artifacts at once: the 7 `Bad request version` lines in `app.log` (3 at 16:17:44, 4 at 16:18:29) are TLS ClientHellos arriving at a plaintext port, and the otherwise-empty `https://host.docker.internal:8080` site entry in `report.json` is where ZAP filed them. So the malformed probes are the alert's **effect**, not its cause — which is the reverse of the obvious reading, and is settled by a field that has to be looked for. *"No artifact establishes this"* is a claim with the same standing as any other and needs the same checking. **All five committed passive alerts reproduced at identical instance counts** (`10038-1`=4, `10020-1`=2, `10036-2`=4, `10021`=2, `10027`=1, total 13), so the passive corpus is unaffected.
- **What survives this entry's `Deferral rationale:` completely intact, and it is the sentence M5.4 is built on.** *"Pointing an active scanner at a user-supplied URL is a materially different act from crawling it"*, and **ADR-013's gate governs which targets are reached, not what is done to them**. Nothing the probe measured touches that. It is why M5.4 puts the `activeScan` job behind explicit per-project **consent**, and why **consent is necessary and not sufficient**: both ADR-013 gates still run, `allow_private_targets` stays test-only, and a consented target that resolves to a private address is still refused. The rationale's other clause — that this "would also need its own decision about scan policy and duration bounds" — is **not discharged by anything yet**: ADR-0024 is M5.4's to write and does not exist.
- **What the probe does NOT establish.** It says nothing about G27: the app was served out-of-process from the checkout itself, which is the one configuration in which the coupling holds by construction, exactly as G27's own Note records for the G23 capture. It also does not establish that this generalises past one Flask app with one `eval` — a second target could fire nothing.

### G25 — A `Scan` records no commit, so nothing in the system says which tree a scan observed
Confirmed: M5.1, active-scan probe · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **M9.1's resolution detection reports resolutions that never happened, and the mechanism is a field nobody added rather than anything the hash does wrong.** M9.1's acceptance criterion 3 makes *"not sighted in scan N"* a missing sighting row, and criterion 4 already narrows that to the tools which succeeded. Neither addresses the tree: with no commit recorded, **a finding absent because it was fixed and a finding absent because the scan ran over a different tree are indistinguishable**, and the system reports the first. That is ADR-0019 decision 3's own principle defeated — *"Where identity is uncertain, prefer the failure that under-counts over the failure that fabricates events"*, which names the fabrication as, verbatim, *"a resolution that never happened"* — both in that decision's own text, not in the ADR's `Consequences`, where an earlier draft of this entry placed it. The whole of that decision's careful work is on `dedup_hash` inputs, which is why it gives no protection here: this arrives through a field that was never added rather than through one that was chosen wrongly. Secondarily, no capture pinned to a commit is reproducible — G23's corpus included.
Evidence: `scanning/domain/scan.py`'s `Scan` is `id, project_id, status, triggered_by, started_at, finished_at, failure_reason` — no commit, and no ORM column or migration adds one. **How it manifests**, and the order matters because the reverse reads as a tooling caveat and gets skipped: `GitRepoCheckout.checkout` runs `git clone --depth 1` with no `--branch` and no `--revision`, so a caller cannot *ask* for a revision; `--depth 1` means a named SHA is not even in the object store unless it is the default-branch tip. G23's capture therefore verified its pin by reading `git rev-parse HEAD` back out of the checkout and refusing to proceed on a mismatch — sound for one deliberate capture, and not a mechanism the pipeline has at all.
**ADR-016 decision 1 names this consequence in its own reasoning**, which is what makes the gap a narrow one rather than an oversight. Fan-out to one job per `(scan, tool)` was rejected because per-job checkout would let Semgrep and Trivy observe different commits, making cross-tool correlation unsound — in that ADR's words, *"silently, with no artifact recording that it happened"*. **That guarantee holds within one `Scan` and has no cross-scan counterpart.** The same "no artifact recording that it happened" is true *between* scans today, and comparing across scans is precisely what M9.1 does.
Deferral rationale: nothing today reads a scan's commit, because nothing yet compares two scans — M9.1 is the first, and adding a column now would decide its diffing semantics ahead of it. The change is also wider than one field: `Scan` gains it, the migration adds it, `RepoCheckoutPort.checkout` has to return the resolved revision (or accept one), and `TriggerScanUseCase` has to decide whether a caller may pin. Trigger: **M9.1**, or any change to `RepoCheckoutPort`, or the next time this corpus needs re-capturing — whichever comes first.
Note: **distinct from G23, G24 and G27 in the G9/G10 manner** — see G24's Note for the separation *(four-way since G27 was added, 2026-08-25)*. This one is the only one of the three that requires a change under `src/`.
Note (2026-08-25, active-scan probe): **a hand-instance of the pinning workaround — and it establishes LESS than it first appears, which is the part worth recording.** The probe needed the same pinned SHA and got it by **full clone followed by `git checkout c68caa7aba8db8ae64da6dd2b4e1b8a05ecd1850`**, first try, no fallback. `probe/target/.git/logs/HEAD` carries both operations in sequence (`clone: from https://github.com/CarlosPD8/verion-demo-target`, then `checkout: moving from main to c68caa7…`). **But `main` *was* `c68caa7` at the time, so the checkout moved no objects and the run never fetched a non-tip commit.** That lands squarely inside the exemption this entry's own `Evidence:` already states — a named SHA "is not even in the object store **unless it is the default-branch tip**". **So what the probe demonstrates is the *verification* step, not the *capability*:** it confirms that reading `git rev-parse HEAD` back out and refusing on a mismatch works, and says nothing whatever about reaching an older commit. Dropping `--depth 1` would make the pin genuinely requestable — a property of git, not something this run showed. **The reflog line reads like a real revision fetch and is not one**, which is the trap worth recording: `checkout: moving from main to c68caa7…` looks identical whether or not the SHA was already the tip. *(`git clone --revision` would do it in one step, but it landed in **git 2.49** and this machine runs **2.39.2**, where the flag is an `unknown option` — so it is a future option rather than an available one, and naming it without the version would send somebody down a path that does not work here.)* **This does not shrink the entry's scope**, and reading it that way would be the mistake: the fix is still `Scan` gaining a field, the migration, `RepoCheckoutPort.checkout` returning or accepting the resolved revision, and `TriggerScanUseCase` deciding whether a caller may pin — the checkout mechanics are the smallest of those four. What it removes is any suggestion that the work is blocked on something git will not do. It also re-fires this entry's own third trigger, *"the next time this corpus needs re-capturing"*: the probe re-fetched the corpus and used the hand mechanism again, which is twice now.

### G26 — `Finding.cwe` for a multi-CWE vulnerability is whichever CWE Trivy happens to list first, and nothing establishes that ordering is stable
Confirmed: M5.1, M6.1 · Status: open
Kind: owed · Blocks: internal
Blocks-if-unresolved: **a production field can change value between scans of an unchanged project, driven only by a vulnerability-database update nobody controls — and both consumers of that field are being designed right now.**
- **M5.1's signal choice.** CWE is a candidate matching signal, so a matcher grouping by CWE would group the same findings differently on consecutive scans of an untouched project. The regrouping would have no cause visible anywhere in the project's own data.
- **M6's use of `cwe`.** The Risk Engine reads it as a scoring input; rule 5 requires scoring to stay traceable to explicit inputs, and an input that silently changes value is traceable to nothing a reader can inspect.
- **What ADR-0018 decision 4 actually assumed.** It chose a single `str | None` over a tuple on the basis that the discarded CWEs were *discardable*. It did not establish — and did not claim — that the *survivor* is deterministic across time. Those are different properties, and only the first was argued.
The field does **not** re-key: `cwe` is excluded from `dedup_hash` (ADR-0019 decision 3), so a flip creates no duplicate row and fabricates no resolution. But `"cwe"` **is** in `_REFRESHED_COLUMNS` in `normalization`'s Postgres repository, so the upsert overwrites the stored value on every re-normalization — the row silently changes and nothing records that it did.
Evidence: Trivy emits `CweIDs` as a **list**; `mappers/trivy.py` takes `next(iter(vulnerability.get("CweIDs") or []), None)`, which is element `[0]` in document order. G23's corpus contains exactly one such case — `CVE-2024-49767` (Werkzeug 2.3.8) with `["CWE-400", "CWE-770"]`, mapped to `CWE-400`. **The corpus cannot even establish which rule is in force**: `CWE-400` is both the first element and the numerically lower one, so "first in document order" and "lowest" are indistinguishable from the data, and only the mapper's code settles it. `mappers/semgrep.py` has the same shape via `_first()` and is inert only while G6 holds; `mappers/zap.py` is immune, since `cweid` is scalar.
Deferral rationale: **it is measurable and cheap, and the exit condition is stated so this does not sit open indefinitely** — map the committed `trivy_scan.json` against two Trivy vulnerability-DB versions and compare the `CweIDs` ordering for `CVE-2024-49767`. Stable ordering closes this entry with a recorded measurement; unstable ordering makes it a real defect and the fix is a deterministic selection rule (lowest numeric CWE, say) rather than positional chance. It is deferred rather than done because the decision it feeds — whether CWE is a matching signal at all — is **G6's** at M5.1, and a selection rule chosen before that decision could be work spent on a field correlation ends up not using. Trigger: **M5.1's signal choice**, or M6, or the next Trivy version bump, whichever is first.
Note: **distinct from G20 and from G6, and the separation is the point.** G20 is about a *fixture measurement* drifting silently when somebody re-captures — a documentation-integrity gap whose fix is a test. G6 is about a signal being *absent* for one tool, whose fix is ruleset metadata. **This is neither: it is a production field whose value is not stable under an input nobody controls**, and its fix is a selection rule in a mapper. G20 is how this was *found* — the same multi-CWE vulnerability that fired G20's guard — but the two entries would be discharged by entirely different work, so folding them would lose one of the two.
